--- Page 1 ---
# CHARITABLE
# AND EASY
# CHEMISTRY,
->For the benefit of Ladies.<-
->By Miss M. M.<- <note>M. M. stands for Marie Meurdrac (c. 1610–1680), a French scientist who wrote this work to make chemistry and pharmacy accessible to women, emphasizing that the mind has no sex.</note>
->SECOND EDITION.<-

<note>A circular library stamp from the French Royal Library is visible, featuring a central crown surrounded by the words "BIBLIOTHEQUE ROYALE."</note>

<note>A small ornamental woodcut depicting a crown is placed as a decorative spacer.</note>

->IN PARIS,<-
->At the shop of JEAN D’HOÜRY, at the Sign of<-
->St. John, on the Augustins Quay.<-
->1674.<- <note>original: "M. DC. LXXIV."</note>
->With Privilege & Approval.<- <note>A "Privilege" was a royal legal grant that gave a printer the exclusive rights to publish a specific book for a set number of years.</note>

<summary>This is the title page of the 1674 second edition of Marie Meurdrac's groundbreaking treatise, which was one of the first chemistry books written by a woman for a female audience. The text highlights the era's transition from alchemy to practical chemistry, focusing on medicinal and cosmetic preparations.</summary>
<keywords>Marie Meurdrac, 17th-century chemistry, women in science, French pharmacy, Jean d’Hoüry, Paris book trade, cosmetics</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the "Epistle Dedicatory," a formal letter of dedication following the title page of Marie Meurdrac’s "Charitable and Easy Chemistry." It establishes the author's social connection to her patron, the Countess de Guiche.</meta>

<note>A rectangular decorative woodcut headpiece serves as a border, featuring two reclining human figures surrounded by intricate foliage and blooming flowers.</note>

# TO THE COUNTESS
# DE GUICHE. <note>original: "GVICHE." The Countess of Guiche was Marie-Louise-Charlotte de Gramont (1623–1694), a prominent noblewoman at the French court and a member of the influential Gramont family.</note>

**M**ADAM,

As soon as I resolved to let this book leave my hands, I was inspired to make of it an offering to you. <note>The original French uses the word "sacrifice," which in 17th-century formal dedications meant a humble tribute or the "offering" of one's work to a social superior.</note> My heart, quite carried away, without reflecting on the greatness of you- <note>The text breaks mid-word at "vo-," which would be completed as "vostre" (your) on the following page.</note>
ã ij <note>This "ã ij" is a signature mark, used by early modern printers to keep the pages in the correct order during binding.</note>

<summary>This page opens the formal dedication of the book to the Countess de Guiche, a common practice for 17th-century authors seeking social legitimacy and protection for their work. Marie Meurdrac expresses her immediate impulse to offer her scientific labor to the Countess as a tribute.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Countess de Guiche, Marie-Louise-Charlotte de Gramont, patronage, 17th-century social customs, women in science</keywords>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to the Countess de Guiche. The first word "stre" completes the word "vostre" (your) from the catchword "vo-" at the bottom of the previous page.</meta>

->## EPISTLE.<-

[your] merit, and on the faults of my Work, [my heart] consented with joy. Reason wished to make me recognize my temerity; it drew for me a Portrait of your perfections so dazzling that I could not consider your illustrious Birth, your great Virtue, and your rare Beauty, except with a respect mixed with fear and veneration. It represented to me the services that your Ancestors rendered to France: the conduct of the Constable of Lesdiguières, <note>François de Bonne, Duke of Lesdiguières (1543–1626), was a celebrated military leader and the last person to hold the title of Constable of France, the highest military rank under the French crown.</note> and the wise administration of the Duke of Sully, <note>Maximilien de Béthune, Duke of Sully (1560–1641), was the chief minister to King Henry IV. He was famous for restoring France's finances and infrastructure after the Wars of Religion.</note> whom the greatest of all Monarchs <note>A reference to King Henry IV of France (r. 1589–1610), who was the Countess's grandfather's close ally and sovereign.</note> was not content merely to reward with the office of Prime Minister, and with that

<summary>In this page of the dedication, Marie Meurdrac extols the Countess de Guiche’s illustrious lineage, specifically mentioning her ancestors Lesdiguières and Sully. She uses the "humility topos," a common literary device where an author contrasts their own "boldness" and the "faults" of their work against the dazzling virtues of their patron.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Marie Meurdrac, Duke of Sully, Constable of Lesdiguières, Henry IV, genealogy, 17th-century nobility, patronage</keywords>

->## EPISTLE<-

rather my respectful intention

these foundations, I take the liberty,
**MADAM**, of presenting to you
this small fruit of my nightly vigils <note>The "fruit of my nightly vigils" (fruit de mes veilles) is a common literary expression of the era, referring to the labor of studying and writing during the quiet hours of the night.</note>: it

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page follows the dedication from Marie Meurdrac to the Countess de Guiche. It features a commendatory poem written by a contemporary, Du Pelletier, praising both the author's scientific skill and her social wisdom in choosing such a prestigious patron.</meta>

<note>A decorative horizontal band of floral ornaments adorns the top of the page, a common typographical feature in 17th-century printing used to introduce a new section of the text.</note>

### TO MISS MEURDRAC,
#### On her Book of Chemistry, dedicated to the Countess de Guiche.

->#### S O N N E T.<-

I admire your learning, and your famous Work
Must be for the Learned a most precious discourse:
All shines in this Book, and the very first page
Shows a subject <note>The "subject" refers to the Countess de Guiche, whose name and virtues appear on the opening page of the dedication.</note> that charms both the heart and the eye.

It knows how to join wisdom to beauty;
It inspires love even in the most envious;
And the finest Minds, by common consent,
Say that in this model one sees the labor of the Heavens.

It is a fitting sign of your illustrious choice
To have as Protectress so august a Person,
Whom Earth and Heaven behold with delight.

Minerva <note>Minerva is the Roman goddess of wisdom, medicine, and the arts; her mention here highlights Meurdrac’s intellectual and scientific achievement.</note> being a friend to sublime virtues,
Doubtless she taught you the art of choosing so well;
For this secret is not one found in Chemistry. <note>Du Pelletier wittily suggests that while Meurdrac knows the secrets of chemical science, the "secret" of finding such a perfect patron came from divine wisdom rather than a laboratory.</note>

->**DU PELLETIER.**<-

ã iiij <note>This is a "signature mark," used by 17th-century bookbinders to ensure the pages were assembled in the correct order.</note>

<summary>A laudatory sonnet by Du Pelletier praising Marie Meurdrac's scientific intellect and her social grace in securing the Countess de Guiche as a patron.</summary>
<keywords>Marie Meurdrac, Du Pelletier, commendatory verse, sonnet, Minerva, 17th-century chemistry, patronage</keywords>

### TO MISS MEURDRAC,
#### On the dedication of her Book.

->#### S I X A I N.<-
<note>A sixain is a poem or stanza consisting of six lines, often used in the 17th century for short, complimentary verses.</note>

Even if you had, through your Works,
Obtained all the rewards
That are justly due to the fruits of your mind, <note>The original French uses "esprit," which encompasses wit, intelligence, and creative spirit.</note>
You would never have received such high praise
As when you dedicated <note>original: "consacré"</note> this writing
To her who possesses the qualities of an Angel. <note>This is a clever double-compliment: it refers to the virtues of the Countess de Guiche (the book’s patron), while playing upon the name of the poem’s author, Angélique Salerne.</note>

->**ANGÉLIQUE SALERNE.**<-

<summary>A six-line complimentary poem by Angélique Salerne praising Marie Meurdrac's intellect and her wise choice of the Countess de Guiche as a patron.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory front matter of Marie Meurdrac’s "La Chymie Charitable et Facile" (Charitable and Easy Chemistry). (likely a male admirer or scholar), who praises Meurdrac for her clarity and generosity in sharing scientific knowledge that previous philosophers had kept hidden.</meta>

# TO MISS MEURDRAC,
## On her *Charitable & Easy Chemistry*.

->### SONNET.<-

Cease, peoples, cease from raising Altars
To those who had only given you some depiction <note>The author refers to the "Ancient" philosophers who provided only a "painting" or "shadow" of the truth rather than clear instructions.</note>
Of the secrets that Nature enclosed in her breast,
And place them no longer in the rank of the Immortals.

No, no, it was wrongly that they were held as such,
Since they have veiled from us in a very dark night <note>This refers to "hermetic" or "occult" writing styles used by earlier alchemists to keep their experiments secret from the uninitiated.</note>
That which God had placed in every creature,
For the common use of the rest of mortals.

We owe to you, MEVRDRAC, rather the homages
That the Ancients once paid to their Sages;
They hid their art from us with too affected a care, <note>original: "soin trop affecté," meaning they went to great lengths to be obscure.</note>

And you, you show us that it is no longer difficult,
And through a spirit full of generosity
Finally you make it charitable and easy for us.

**P. D. L.**

<note>The notation "ã v" at the bottom of the page is a signature mark, an alphabetic and numeric code used by 17th-century printers to ensure the folded sheets of paper (quires) were assembled in the correct order for binding.</note>

<summary>A complimentary sonnet by "P. D. L." praising Marie Meurdrac for democratizing chemistry and criticizing the intentional obscurity of ancient male philosophers.</summary>
<keywords>Marie Meurdrac, sonnet, alchemy, history of chemistry, women in science, transparency, 17th-century poetry</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the preliminary material for Marie Meurdrac’s "Charitable and Easy Chemistry." Following the previous sonnet by P. D. L., which praised Meurdrac for her clarity and generosity, this page presents a "prosopopoeia"—a rhetorical device where an absent or imaginary person is represented as speaking. Here, the speaker is the god Apollo, who in Greek and Roman mythology was the patron of both medicine and the sun.</meta>

<note>A decorative border of floral ornaments, including stylized leaves and blooms, frames the top of the text, signifying the start of a new honorary poem.</note>

# APOLLO’S PROSOPOPOEIA,
<note>From the Greek *prosopopoieia*: a figure of speech in which an imagined or absent person, or even an inanimate object, is represented as speaking. In this context, the poet writes from the perspective of the god Apollo.</note>

## On the Work of Mademoiselle MEURDRAC.

->### SONNET.<-

Medicine comes from my invention,
And I am, after God, the Operator of the world: <note>As the god of the Sun, Apollo was considered the "Operator" because solar heat was believed to be the primary force driving all natural growth and chemical transformations on Earth.</note>
My power has no equal anywhere,
And all things are born from my action alone.

Herbs and fruits are my production:
The animals in the air, on the earth, and in the wave:
I make minerals by my fertile virtue, <note>original: "vertu feconde." In the 17th century, "virtue" referred to the inherent power or medicinal property of a substance.</note>
And the King of metals in its perfection. <note>A standard alchemical term for gold, which was associated with the Sun/Apollo.</note>

I have among Mortals for Companions two women,
Of whom the illustrious de GUICHE, by stealing my flames, <note>This refers to Suzanne de Pons, Countess de Guiche, the patron to whom Marie Meurdrac dedicated her book. The "flames" represent both the light of the sun and the heat of the chemical furnace.</note>
Shines in the Court like another Sun:

And the learned MEURDRAC in her style of writing,
By imitating my Art, shares my Empire:
And not one of us three finds their equal.

**I. D. S. N.**

<summary>A laudatory sonnet written from the perspective of the god Apollo, who claims Marie Meurdrac and her patron, the Countess de Guiche, as his earthly equals in the arts of medicine and chemistry.</summary>
<keywords>Apollo, Prosopopoeia, Countess de Guiche, King of metals, gold, solar heat, Marie Meurdrac, 17th-century poetry</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory poems praising Marie Meurdrac. Following the poem where the god Apollo yields to her skill, this sonnet by "Du Pelletier" (likely a contemporary scholar or admirer) focuses on the rarity of a woman achieving such scientific heights and the intellectual "secrets" she has revealed.</meta>

<note>A decorative border of floral and ornamental motifs, including swirling leaves and blossoms, frames the top of the poem.</note>

# To
# MADEMOISELLE MEURDRAC,
## On her Book of Chemistry.

->### SONNET.<-

A hundred times I have celebrated the most sublime deeds
Of the famous Heroes of whom History boasts:
But when I must speak of an illustrious learned woman, <note>original: "Sçavante." In the 17th century, this term was used for a woman of great scientific or scholarly knowledge, though often with a degree of surprise from male contemporaries.</note>
Either I speak in trembling, MEURDRAC, or I keep silent.

Your book makes us see marvelous effects,
Which have surpassed the expectations of even the most envious;
Whether your spirit imitates nature, or whether it invents,
The seeker finds therein the end of his desires. <term>seeker: the original "curieux" refers to a 17th-century amateur or scholar who studied the "curiosities" of nature and science.</term>

How much glory our descendants will one day give you: <note>original: "Neveux." Literally "nephews," but in the poetic language of the time, it refers to posterity or future generations.</note>
And to tell the truth, it will be hard to believe
That a woman ever possessed so much knowledge.

Of the secrets that your Book explains on every
page,
To hide nothing from you, I would wish to have
Only the secret of worthily praising your Work.

DU PELLETIER.
ã vj <note>This is a signature mark, used by 17th-century printers to keep the pages of a book in the correct order for binding.</note>

<summary>An honorary sonnet by Du Pelletier praising Marie Meurdrac for her scientific expertise and the clarity of her writing, while reflecting the era's surprise at a woman's mastery of chemistry.</summary>
<keywords>Marie Meurdrac, Du Pelletier, women in science, 17th-century chemistry, commendatory verse, posterity, scientific secrets</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of laudatory poems following Du Pelletier's sonnet. This second sonnet, signed by "M.lle D. I.," shifts the focus from general scientific acclaim to the specific medicinal and cosmetic applications of Meurdrac’s work, particularly her dedication to the Countess of Guiche.</meta>

<note>A decorative border of floral patterns and ornamental foliage frames the top of the text.</note>

->### SONNET.<-

Tell us what Spirit, or what Deity,
Inspires the knowledge seen in your Work,
And who gave you this beautiful art as your share
Of prolonging life’s days, and restoring health?

You know, you say, how to preserve beauty,
And repair the damage of defects in the complexion; <term>complexion: "tein" in the original, referring to the color and texture of the skin, a major focus of 17th-century cosmetic chemistry.</term>
But from the gallant Sex, I promise you homage, <note>original: "du Sexe galand." In this period, the "gallant sex" was a common polite reference to women.</note>
If you have found this much-desired secret.

Not that they hope to possess that delicacy
Which is seen shining in the complexion of the lovely Countess
To whom you dedicate this perfect Work. <note>Marie Meurdrac dedicated her book, *La Chymie Charitable et Facile, en faveur des Dames*, to the Comtesse de Guiche.</note>

The vainest among us would wish herself less beautiful,
And your finest secrets will have enough effect
If they allow us to follow, from afar, this illustrious Model.

M.LLE D. I.

<summary>A commendatory sonnet by an anonymous woman (M.lle D. I.) praising Marie Meurdrac's ability to preserve health and beauty through chemistry. The poem highlights the work's dedication to the Countess of Guiche, presenting her as the ideal standard of beauty.</summary>
<keywords>Marie Meurdrac, cosmetics, health, beauty, Countess of Guiche, 17th-century women in science, sonnet, complexion</keywords>

->FIRST PART.<-

PREFACE.

Of Salt, page 1 <note>Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury were known as the *tria prima* (three primes), the foundational elements of Paracelsian chemistry which Meurdrac utilizes here.</note>
Of Sulfur, 3
Of Mercury, 4
Of Chemical Operations, 8
Of Distillations.
Distillation by ascent, 9 <note>original: "per ascensum." A process where vapors rise and condense above the source of heat.</note>
Distillation with filings, sand & ashes, 12 <note>These materials were used as heat baths to provide steady, indirect temperature control for glass vessels.</note>
Distillation in the Bain-Marie, 13 <note>A water bath, traditionally named after the legendary alchemist Mary the Jewess.</note>
Steam bath, 14
Distillation by the "horned vessel," 15 <note>original: "per medium cornutum," referring to a retort, a flask with a long downward-pointing neck used for distillation.</note>
Distillation by descent, 17 <note>original: "per descensum." A method where heat is applied from above and the distilled liquid is forced downward through a grate.</note>
Sublimation, 18 <note>A process of heating a solid until it turns into a gas and then solidifies again, used to purify substances like sulfur or mercury.</note>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, this section concludes the Table of Contents for the first part of the treatise. It moves from general principles and distillation methods into a comprehensive list of chemical operations, laboratory equipment, and the management of heat.</meta>

Table of Chapters.

Rectification, 19 <note>The process of purifying a liquid by distilling it multiple times to increase its strength and clarity.</note>
Calcination, 20 <note>Reducing a solid substance to a fine powder or ash by subjecting it to prolonged, intense heat.</note>
Cohobation, 20 <note>A technique where the distilled liquid is poured back onto the solid residue (the "dregs") and redistilled to further extract its virtues.</note>
Coagulation, 21 <note>The process of thickening a liquid or turning it into a solid state.</note>
Filtration, 22 <note>Straining a liquid through a porous material, such as paper or cloth, to remove impurities.</note>
Desiccation, 22 <note>The thorough drying of a substance.</note>
Dulcoration, 22 <note>Literally "sweetening"; the process of washing a substance with water to remove salts or acidic vapors.</note>
Inclination, 23 <note>Also known as decanting; carefully pouring off a clear liquid so that the sediment remains undisturbed at the bottom of the vessel.</note>
Amalgamation, 23 <note>The mixing of a metal, like gold or silver, with mercury to create a soft, paste-like consistency.</note>
Digestion, 23 <note>Applying gentle, constant heat to a substance in a sealed vessel over a long period, mimicking the natural warmth of the stomach.</note>
Putrefaction, 24 <note>The decomposition of matter through warmth and moisture, often seen as a necessary stage of rebirth in alchemical philosophy.</note>
Menstruum, 25 <note>original: "Menstruë." A solvent used to dissolve solids; the name refers to the alchemical belief in the cyclic, monthly nature of such reactions.</note>
Fermentation, 25
Circulation, 26 <note>A process where a liquid is heated so that its vapors rise, condense, and fall back into the original liquid in a continuous cycle.</note>
Deliquescence, 27 <note>original: "Defaillance." The process by which a solid substance absorbs enough moisture from the air to dissolve into a liquid.</note>
Reverberation, 27 <note>A method of heating where flames are reflected or "reverberated" off the arched roof of a furnace onto the substance below.</note>
Precipitation, 27 <note>The process of causing a solid substance to separate and fall to the bottom of a liquid solution.</note>
Saltwater bath, 28 <note>original: "Eau marinée." The use of brine or salted water as a heating medium for controlled temperature.</note>
Stratification, 28 <note>Arranging different materials in alternating layers, often within a crucible for heating.</note>
Torrefaction, 29 <note>The roasting or parching of a substance over a fire to dry it or make it brittle.</note>
Decrepitation, 30 <note>The crackling or popping sound made by certain salts when they are heated and their internal moisture expands.</note>
Dregs, 30 <note>original: "Feces." The solid residue or sediment that remains after a process.</note>
Dead Head, 31 <note>original: "Teste morte" or "Caput Mortuum." The inert, useless material left behind after all the chemical "spirits" have been extracted.</note>
Of vessels, 31 <note>A discussion of the various glassware and ceramic containers used in the laboratory.</note>
Of the luting for vessels, 33 <note>Sealants—often made of clay, hair, or egg whites—used to make the joints of laboratory equipment airtight.</note>
Of fires, 35 <note>Instruction on the different grades and types of heat required for chemical operations.</note>
Of furnaces, 36

<summary>This page concludes the Table of Contents for the first part of the manual, listing essential laboratory operations, tools, and heat management techniques.</summary>
<keywords>Table of Contents, Laboratory operations, Calcination, Digestion, Menstruum, Laboratory vessels, Luting, Furnaces, Alchemical terminology</keywords>

->### FOURTH PART.<-

->FOREWORD. 153<-
Of vitriol, 155 <term>Vitriol: A general term for metallic sulfates (like iron or copper sulfate), prized in early chemistry for creating powerful acids and medicinal salts.</term>
Of the spirit of nitre, 158 <note>Commonly known as nitric acid, produced by distilling saltpeter with vitriol.</note>
Of mineral crystal, 159 <note>original: "cristal mineral." A preparation of purified saltpeter (potassium nitrate) fused into a transparent mass, used to treat fevers and sore throats.</note>
Of the spirit of sea salt, 161 <note>Now known as hydrochloric acid.</note>
Of the spirit of sulfur, 163 <note>Produced by capturing the fumes of burning sulfur, this was a form of sulfuric acid.</note>
Syrup of flowers of sulfur, 165 <term>Flowers of sulfur: A fine powder obtained by sublimation (heating sulfur into vapor and letting it condense). It was frequently used for respiratory issues.</term>
Of the oil of the Philosophers, 166 <note>original: "l'huile des Philosophes." A traditional alchemical remedy made by distilling bricks soaked in olive oil; it was used topically for joint pain and "cold" diseases.</note>
Of the essence of karabe, or amber, 168 <term>Karabe: An old name for amber (from the Persian "kahrubā"). It was distilled to produce an oil believed to be effective against hysteria and convulsions.</term>
Of the tincture of coral, 168
Of the magistery of coral, 169 <term>Magistery: A chemical term for a precipitate formed from a solution. Magistery of coral was often used as an antacid or to strengthen the heart.</term>
Of the salt of coral, 170
Of the crocus of antimony, 171 <term>Crocus: In early chemistry, this referred to a reddish or yellow-orange powder made from a metal. Crocus of antimony was a potent emetic (causing vomiting).</term>
Of diaphoretic antimony, 173 <note>original: "antimoine duphoretique." A chemical preparation of antimony and saltpeter designed to induce sweating without the violent vomiting usually associated with antimony.</note>
Of the oil or syrup of antimony, 174
Of the tincture of antimony, 175
Of the crocus of Mars, 177 <term>Crocus of Mars: A reddish-brown iron oxide (rust) prepared chemically, used as a tonic to treat "green sickness" (anemia) and other blood disorders.</term>

->### *Extract of the Royal Privilege* ###<-

BY grace & Privilege of the King, it is permitted to Lady <note>original: "Damoiselle." In the 17th century, this title was used for women of the bourgeoisie or minor nobility; it did not necessarily imply she was unmarried.</note> MARIE MEURDRAC to have printed a Book she has composed, titled *Charitable and Easy Chemistry, etc.* <note>The full title is *La Chymie Charitable & facile, en faveur des dames* (Easy and Charitable Chemistry, for the Benefit of Ladies).</note> during the space of ten years: with prohibitions to all others from printing, selling or distributing it during the said time, without the consent of the said Petitioner or of those who shall have right from her, under the penalties set forth more fully by the said Privilege. Given at Paris the 20th of December 1665. Signed, DE QUIGY.

And the said Lady Marie Meurdrac has ceded & transferred her right of Privilege for the time & according to the clauses it contains, to Jean d’Hoüry, Merchant Bookseller, <note>In this period, a "bookseller" (libraire) often acted as the publisher, managing the production and legal registration of the work.</note> following the agreement made between them.

*Registered in the Book of the Community, the 5th of January 1666. Signed,* PIGET.

---

->### *Approval of the Doctors of Medicine* ###<-

WE the undersigned Regent Doctors <note>Regent Doctors were senior members of the faculty authorized to teach and govern the medical school.</note> in the Faculty of Medicine at Paris, certify having seen & read a Book titled *Charitable and Easy Chemistry*: Composed by Lady MARIE MEUDRAC <note>The name is spelled "MEUDRAC" here, likely a printer's error for Meurdrac.</note> in which we find nothing that may not be useful to the Public. In witness whereof we have subscribed. Done this 10th of December 1665.

->Signed, LE VIGNON, Dean.<-
->DE CAEN. DE BOURGES.<-

->### PREFACE ###<-

<note>The text begins with a large decorative initial 'Q'.</note> **W**HEN I began this little Treatise, it was for my sole satisfaction, and so as not to lose the memory of the knowledge I acquired through long labor and various experiments repeated many times. I cannot hide that, seeing it finished better than I had dared to hope, I was tempted to publish it: but if I had reasons to bring it to light, I also had reasons to keep it hidden, and not expose it to general censure. In this struggle, I remained for nearly two years irresolute: I objected to myself that it was not the profession of a woman to teach; that she should remain in silence, listen and learn, without showing that she knows: that it is above her to give a Work to the public, and that this <note>The sentence continues onto the next page, where Meurdrac likely discusses the reputational risks for women authors in the 17th century.</note>

## Vessels, Lutes, Furnaces,
## Fires, Characters & Weights of

### CHAPTER ONE.
### Of Salt.

**C**hemistry has for its object mixed bodies <term>mixed bodies</term>: in 17th-century science, these were substances composed of several elements or principles, which chemistry aimed to separate and purify. insofar as they are divisible and resolvable, upon which it works to extract the three Principles, which are Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury <note>These three principles, known as the *Tria Prima*, were popularized by Paracelsus. They were seen not just as common substances, but as the fundamental spiritual and physical components of all matter: Salt represented body and stability, Sulfur represented soul and flammability, and Mercury represented spirit and volatility.</note>; this is accomplished through two general operations, namely Solution and Congelation <note>Solution refers to the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid; Congelation refers to the thickening or solidifying of a substance. Together, they represent the fundamental alchemical cycle of *solve et coagula* (dissolve and coagulate).</note>. First, we shall speak of Salt as the father of generation, since it seems...

2	*Chemistry*

that it is this principle which contributes the most to production. There are three kinds found in every body: THE FIXED, THE NITRE, AND THE AMMONIAC <note>These three salts represent different states of stability and volatility. Fixed salt remains in the ashes after burning; Nitre (saltpeter) is associated with growth and spirit; Ammoniac salt is highly volatile and rises during heating.</note>, which proceed from only one source, and are diversified by the mixture of the two other principles <note>The "two other principles" refer to Sulfur and Mercury, mentioned on the previous page.</note>. Fixed Salt is that which is made visible by Art <term>Art</term>: in this period, "the Art" refers specifically to the practice of chemistry or alchemy., and which contains within itself a balsamic virtue <term>balsamic virtue</term>: a healing, soothing, or preservative quality thought to prevent decay. It dissolves in water, it thickens in the heat; and after several dissolutions and purifications, it is made fusible <term>fusible</term>: capable of being melted. like metal, and like balm; and finally, it has the virtue of preserving all things in which it dominates, it purifies them, and dissipates their superfluous humidity; this is why, depending on the compound <term>mixed body</term>: a substance made of different components. from which it is extracted, it performs admirable operations.

The Nitre Salt holds the middle ground between the Fixed and the Ammoniac: it attaches itself to Sulfur, it is present in small quantities, it is not visible, and it preserves the virtue of its subject. If it is extracted from a Purgative, it will purge; from a Diuretic, it will cause urination, and so for the rest. It is this Salt which gives the taste and smell to Sulfur.

The Ammoniac is that which passes with

## CHAPTER II.
*Of Sulfur.*

**Sulfur**, the second principle, creates the union of the spirit and the body; this is why it is called the **Soul** by some authors <note>In the "Tria Prima" (Three Principles) theory of Paracelsus, Salt represents the Body, Sulfur the Soul, and Mercury the Spirit.</note>. There are three kinds: gross, medium, and subtle. The gross joins with Fixed Salt, the medium with Nitre Salt, and the subtle with [Salt] Ammoniac. The gross is present in small quantity, and yet it is what gives the balsamic virtue <term>balsamic virtue</term>: a healing, soothing, or preservative quality thought to prevent decay and maintain the integrity of a substance. to the Fixed Salt.

The medium Sulfur is composed of fatty, hot, and oily parts; it burns easily, it is visible by its effects, it dominates the Nitre Salt, and joins with it.

->A ij<- <note>This is a signature mark, an alphanumeric code used by early modern printers to ensure the folded sheets of paper were bound in the correct sequence.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on how salts preserve distilled waters and introduces Sulfur as the 'Soul' that unites body and spirit. Meurdrac categorizes Sulfur into three types—gross, medium, and subtle—and explains their relationship to different salts.</summary>
<keywords>Sulfur, Soul, balsamic virtue, distillation, chemical principles, Marie Meurdrac, Salt Ammoniac</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter II, "Of Sulfur," following the categorization of Sulfur into gross and medium types. Meurdrac concludes the section on Sulfur by describing the "subtle" type and its extraction from plants before beginning Chapter III on Mercury.</meta>

4 *Chemistry*

Subtle Sulfur unites with Salt Ammoniac; it is this Sulfur that facilitates its elevation <note>In alchemy, "elevation" refers to the process of volatilization or sublimation, where a substance is raised by heat into a vaporous state.</note>, and which causes the Spirits to ignite.

The oily and fatty parts, which we call Sulfur, are not extracted from the same part of a mixture <note>original: "le mixte." In early chemistry, this refers to any natural compound or substance composed of the three principles (Salt, Sulfur, Mercury).</note>, nor are they present in all things in equal measure. In some, they are found in the roots and bark; in others, in the flowers, leaves, or fruits; and in some, in the seeds. This Sulfur is easily corrupted, as it dominates the Nitre Salt. This is seen in oils extracted by expression <term>expression</term>: the process of squeezing or pressing a substance to extract its juices or oils, such as pressing olives., which grow thick and putrefy; this does not occur with distilled essences, as they are aided and refined by fire.

***

### *Of Mercury.*

ALL things in the world originate from one, and this one produces three <note>Meurdrac alludes to the Hermetic concept of "All is One" and the Paracelsian "Tria Prima" (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury), suggesting that all matter derives from a single primordial substance (the *Prima Materia*) which then manifests as three distinct principles.</note>: which can...

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Chapter III, "Of Mercury," from page 4. Meurdrac is drawing a philosophical parallel between the Holy Trinity and the three alchemical principles (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury), while beginning to categorize the three types of Mercury.</meta>

Charitable & Easy. 5

...give an idea of the adorable mystery of the most holy Trinity. Chemistry presents us with a sketch <note>original: "un crayon." In this period, a "crayon" referred to a preliminary drawing or a rough outline used to illustrate a concept.</note> of it, since it finds a trinity not only in every subject <note>In alchemical writing, a "subject" refers to any substance or matter under study.</note>, but in every principle. We have seen in the preceding chapters three sorts of Salt and three conditions of Sulfur; there are also certainly three different Mercuries, which—as I said in the Chapter on Salts—proceed from only one, and are diversified only by the mixture of the two other principles.

There is found in the Fixed Salt <term>fixed salt</term>: a salt that remains stable and does not vaporize even when subjected to intense heat., when joined to coarse Sulfur, a heavy Mercury, stubborn <note>original: "rebelle." Alchemists described substances that were difficult to distill or transform as "rebellious."</note> and difficult to elevate <note>In alchemy, "elevation" refers to the process of sublimation or distillation, where heat causes a substance to rise as vapor.</note>, which only releases by the force of fire. We see this in the distillation of the Salt of Simples <note>Simples are individual medicinal plants or herbs.</note>, from which the Mercury is only drawn by a long and pressing fire. The Spirit of Salt <note>Generally refers to the acidic liquid—hydrochloric acid—produced by distilling salt.</note> is therefore nothing other than a heavy Mercury united to the Fixed Salt, which contains within itself a coarse Sulfur, where the Sulfur and Mercury are dominated by the Fixed Salt.

An infinite number of Philosophers have written of Mercury according to their own sentiment, and each in particular has followed their own inclination rather than reason. Some maintain that the Mercury of simples <note>Medicinal plants or herbs.</note> and of animals should be flowing and shining <note>original: "coulant & lucide." This refers to the silvery, liquid appearance of metallic quicksilver.</note> like that of metals; which cannot be, as there is a very great difference between the three kingdoms <note>The three kingdoms of nature: Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral.</note>. Others maintain that it should be subtle, diaphanous, transparent, and insipid <note>Tasteless.</note>; which has no appearance of truth. I agree that it must be very clear, very subtle, and detached from all acrimony <note>In 17th-century chemistry, "acrimony" referred to a harsh, corrosive, or biting quality in a substance.</note>, but not insipid, since it is this spirit of life, which is all the more penetrating as it is more detac- <note>The word "détaché" (detached or separated) is cut off at the end of the page.</note>

## CHAPTER IV.

### *Of Chemical Operations.*

It is necessary that a Craftsman <note>original: "Artisan." In the 17th century, a chemist was considered a skilled artisan or practitioner of a "noble art."</note> know how to speak in the terms of their Art, and that they know the tools and utensils they must use. We will speak first of Operations, and particularly of Distillations.

Chemists perform three kinds of Distillations, which they call: by ascent, by a curved vessel, and by descent <note>original: "Per ascensum, Per medium cornutum, Per descensum." These Latin terms describe the direction in which the distilled vapor travels.</note>. Distillation "by ascent" <note>original: "Per ascensum."</note> is a Distillation that raises the spirits in the form of smoke, where, finding no out-

<summary>Meurdrac argues that practical experimentation is superior to theoretical speculation and begins a technical guide to laboratory operations, starting with the three methods of distillation.</summary>
<keywords>theory vs practice, distillation, Per ascensum, Per descensum, Per medium cornutum, laboratory practice, chemical terminology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 8, explaining how vapors condense in the head of the distilling apparatus before moving on to describe specific methods of distillation.</meta>

Charitable & easy. 9

<note>...out-</note>let, they condense into water, and fall through the canal of the alembic head <note>original: "chapiteau." The 'head' or 'cap' of the still where the vapors collect and cool.</note>. Distillation "by the curved vessel" <note>original: "Per medium cornutum." This refers to using a retort, a vessel with a long, downward-curving neck that acts as a middle ground between rising and falling distillation.</note> is a means or medium for substances that cannot rise easily. Distillation "by descent" <note>original: "Per descensum."</note> is for heavy substances. But let us speak of each of these Distillations in particular.

### Distillation by ascent.
<note>original: "Distillation per ascensum."</note>

Distillation by ascent <note>original: "per ascensum."</note> is performed in several ways, depending on the substance one wishes to distill. If it is Spirit of Wine <note>original: "Esprit de vin." This is a 17th-century term for high-proof alcohol or ethanol.</note>, it must be done in a Water Bath <note>original: "Bain." Short for *bain-marie*, a vessel of hot water used to provide gentle, steady heat.</note>. If one is making aromatic Essences, such as Rosemary, Sage, Hyssop, Fennel, Anise, and others of a similar nature, this distillation can only be performed in a copper Alembic <note>A classic distilling apparatus; Meurdrac specifies copper here because it can withstand much higher heat than the glass of the period.</note>, because a violent fire is required, as well as a great quantity of herbs to extract a single ounce of Essence; furthermore, one cannot find glass vessels large enough that also possess a cooling pipe <note>original: "tuyau refrigeratoire." A condenser, usually a coil of pipe through which the vapor passes to be cooled back into liquid.</note>, even though cooling is necessary to prevent the loss of the spirits. This is why those who have said that it was not absolutely...

Reasoning would lead one to believe, as aromatics are of a hot nature, that the heat of the boiling Bath <note>original: "Bain." You will separate them using a separating vessel, which is a glass funnel that you will fill, plugging the bottom end with your finger; all the Essence will rise to the top, and when no more rises, release your finger and let all the water pass through.

One can distill roses in this way using a gentle fire.

### ->Sublimation<-

MANY have confused Distillation with Sublimation; it is quite true that in both, there is an elevation of fluid and liquid parts, which is why it is called distillation, insofar as the fumes that rise fall back as water: it is not the same for Sublimation, for it is the most spiritual, light, and dry parts that rise and adhere to the neck of the vessel. It is noteworthy that when one sublimes mineral or metallic things, the highest-rising parts, like "wild flour" <note>original: "folle farine." This refers to the finest, most volatile dust or powder produced during sublimation; in the case of minerals like arsenic or mercury, this dust was extremely toxic</note>, are poisonous, and Medicine absolutely forbids their use.

Sublimation is performed simply, or by

<summary>This page concludes the instructions for distillation by descent and defines sublimation as the elevation of dry, "spiritual" particles rather than liquid ones, warning of the toxicity of mineral sublimates.</summary>

<keywords>distillation by descent, rectification, sublimation, wild flour, mineral toxicity, alchemical vessels</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of sublimation from page 18, detailing the types of sublimation and the specific vessels required, before moving on to the process of rectification.</meta>

Charitable & Easy. 19
addition. Simple sublimation occurs when the matter sublimes on its own; the other <note>referring to compound sublimation</note> is when one must add iron filings, prepared Salt, or calcined pebbles to trap the more earthy and coarse parts. The vessel for Sublimation is a Matrass <term>Matrass: a long-necked glass flask</term> or a tall Cucurbit <term>Cucurbit: the lower, gourd-shaped part of a still</term>, with a "blind" alembic <term>blind alembic: a still-head without a spout, used when the goal is to collect solid vapors rather than liquid runoff</term> pierced at the top, or a glass vessel called an Aludel <term>Aludel: a series of pear-shaped pots used as condensers in the sublimation process</term>: the fire must be made of sand, filings, or ashes in a pan, as has been previously described. Remember that it is necessary in all operations to maintain the proper degrees of the fire.

### ->Rectification<-

**T**HIS Operation is very necessary to make spirits purer and more spiritual <note>In this context, "spiritual" refers to the volatility and refinement of a substance, rather than a religious quality</note>, and to detach them from whatever impurities and earthiness they might still retain after having been distilled either by the Retort or by the Alembic. You will rectify in a Bain-Marie <term>Bain-Marie: a water bath used to provide gentle, even heat</term> with a gentle or boiling fire, depending on the nature of the substance. The more you rectify, the more you must diminish the fire; for the parts that rise will be increasingly spiritual.

<summary>This page describes simple and compound sublimation techniques, the specific glassware required, and introduces rectification as the essential process for further purifying distilled spirits.</summary>

<keywords>sublimation, matrass, cucurbit, blind alembic, aludel, rectification, spirits, purification, Bain-Marie, Marie Meurdrac</keywords>

20 *Chemistry*

### ->*Calcination*<-

**T**here are two types of Calcination; one simple, the other corrosive; and both are only for Minerals and Metals. Simple calcination is done by putting your metal or mineral in a Crucible <term>Crucible: a ceramic or metal container used to melt substances at very high temperatures</term>, and giving it a strong fire until you have reduced said Metal or Mineral to an impalpable <note>So fine that the individual grains cannot be felt</note> powder. Corrosive calcination is done by strong waters <note>original: "eaux fortes"; usually refers to nitric acid or other mineral acids</note>, which reduces Metals to calx <term>Calx: the crumbly, oxidized powder remaining after a metal has been roasted or chemically treated</term>. The name Calcination is very poorly given to this Operation, since Calcination is destruction; and gold and silver are not destroyed by strong waters, but only corroded and altered; since they recover their body <note>meaning they return to their solid metallic form</note> under violent fire, it is not Calcination, but only imperfect dissolution.

### ->*Cohobation*<-

**T**O COHOBATE is nothing other than putting that which has been distilled back upon what remains at the bottom of the distilling vessel, so that the spiritual part <note>The volatile or refined essence of the substance</note> penetrates the mass more easily, and so that it...

<summary>This page defines two forms of calcination—simple heat and corrosive acids—and begins explaining cohobation as a method of repeated distillation to enhance the penetration of a substance.</summary>
<keywords>calcination, corrosive calcination, crucible, strong waters, calx, gold and silver, cohobation, distillation, alchemical operations</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the definition of cohobation from page 20, specifically discussing the heat sources required, before moving on to define the process of coagulation.</meta>

Charitable & Easy. 21
increases in potency <note>original: "vertu"; in this context, it refers to the chemical strength or medicinal efficacy of the substance</note>. This Operation is performed in a Water Bath <term>Water Bath: also known as a bain-marie; a vessel of water used to heat another container gently and evenly</term> more than by any other means. For that which is performed in a Retort <term>Retort: a glass vessel with a long, downward-slanting neck used for distilling liquids</term>, the fire must be gentle, for fear of giving the distilled substances a foul odor.

### ->*Coagulation*<-

**T**O COAGULATE is to bring a liquid substance to a firm consistency. It is performed in two ways: one by fire, the other by cold. That of fire is done when you have extracted some tincture; you shall evaporate its moisture in an earthenware pan or glass vessel until it reaches the consistency of honey or an Extract, according to the use for which you wish to employ it. That which is performed by cold is for dissolved salts; once you have evaporated two-thirds of the water and a small film <note>original: "pellicule"</note> forms on the surface, you shall place your vessel in the cold of the cellar, and a portion will coagulate into crystals. These you will finish drying in a Crucible <term>Crucible: a ceramic or metal container capable of withstanding very high temperatures</term> and keep in a well-sealed vessel. Evaporate [the remaining liquid] as described above, and place it in the cold until no more crystals are formed.

<summary>This page concludes the instructions for cohobation, emphasizing gentle heat, and details the two methods of coagulation: using evaporation to create thick extracts and using cooling to crystallize salts.</summary>
<keywords>cohobation, coagulation, distillation, water bath, retort, crystallization, chemical extracts, salts, laboratory equipment</keywords>

22	Chemistry

### *Filtration.*

TO FILTER, in common terms, is to pass or strain a substance to make it clearer and purer. It is performed through grey paper <note>original: "papier gris"; an early form of unsized filter paper</note>, or the Hippocras bag <term>Hippocras bag: a conical fabric filter, named after the Greek physician Hippocrates, used for straining liquids</term>, or by the wick <note>original: "languette"; a method of capillary filtration where a strip of cloth or "tongue" of fabric draws liquid out of one vessel into another, leaving sediment behind</note>, as many times as the operator deems necessary. This Operation is very necessary for the extraction of Tinctures and Salts.

### *Desiccation.*

CALCINATION and Desiccation are nearly the same thing, differing only in degree <note>original: "à la reſerve du plus ou du moins"; literally "to the reservation of more or less," referring to the intensity of the heat applied</note>. Desiccation is for drying and making the material capable of absorbing the liquid that will be added to it, or of being impregnated by said liquid so that it may be easier to grind and pound.

### *Dulcoration.*

TO DULCORATE <term>Dulcorate: from the Latin 'dulcis' (sweet); in chemistry, this means to "sweeten" a substance by washing away corrosive salts or acids with water</term> is to wash the Calx <term>Calx: the powdery or friable residue left after a metal has been roasted or treated with acid</term> of Metals to remove the corrosion that strong waters <term>Strong waters: "eaux fortes," generally referring to nitric acid or other mineral acids used to dissolve metals</term> may have imparted to them. It also serves to dulcorate the Sulfur of Metals and Mi- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; it likely continues as "Minerals" on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page defines three essential chemical processes: filtration for clarifying liquids, desiccation for drying materials to improve their absorbency or grindability, and the beginning of dulcoration for washing acids from metallic residues.</summary>
<keywords>filtration, desiccation, dulcoration, calx, strong waters, Hippocras bag, chemical instruments</keywords>

### Inclination.

INCLINATION is performed when the substance has been washed and dulcorated, and has settled to the bottom of the vessel; one pours off the water from the top by inclination <note>original: "par inclination"; this is the process we now call decantation, where liquid is carefully poured off to leave the sediment undisturbed</note>, which separates from the material easily.

### Amalgamation.

WITHOUT seeking the origin of this word, which according to some Authors, refers only to the mixture of quicksilver <term>Quicksilver: an archaic name for mercury</term> with the Calx <term>Calx: the powdery residue left after a metal has been roasted or treated with acid</term> of Metals; I say that to amalgamate is to mix, incorporate, and grind a mass with a liquid in such a way that they cannot be separated.

### Digestion.

DIGESTION explains itself. It is nothing other than a gentle, penetrating, and digest- <note>original: "dige"; the text is cut off at the bottom of the page, likely intended to be "digestive." Digestion in alchemy refers to the slow application of heat to a substance over a long period, mimicking the heat of a stomach or a nesting bird.</note>

### *Putrefaction.*

**PUTREFACTION** and Digestion are one and the same thing, with the exception that one is longer than the other, and they have two different purposes. Digestion is a coction <term>Coction: a slow "cooking" or boiling process used to ripen or refine a substance.</term>, and Putrefaction is a rotting, intended so that the matter may change its taste, smell, and color in order to produce something more perfect, stripped of all its bad qualities; and this Putrefaction is a preparation for a new generation <note>In the alchemical worldview, "generation" refers to the birth of a new substance; just as a seed must rot in the earth to grow, chemical matter must "die" through putrefaction before it can be transformed.</note>. It is performed in manure or in the Water Bath, adding...

### Menstruum.

**MENSTRUUM** is a liquid which serves to help pull and extract all sorts of spirits, essences, tinctures, and salts; to digest and corrupt all things that have need of moisture. There are several kinds of them; according to the nature of the thing that one wants to extract or corrupt, one uses spirit of wine <term>Spirit of wine: high-proof alcohol or ethanol, used as a powerful solvent.</term>, spirit of dew <note>Dew was often collected by alchemists because it was believed to contain "astral" or celestial energy from the heavens.</note>, strong waters <term>Strong waters (eaux fortes): generally refers to nitric acid or other corrosive mineral acids used to dissolve metals.</term>, lemon juices, distilled vinegar, common water, or distilled water, as the Artist judges appropriate.

### Fermentation.

TAKE the things that you wish to ferment, and crush them if they are green <note>That is, fresh or living plant material.</note>; and if they are dry, they must be moistened and put in a glass vessel that you shall seal, and place in the cellar in sand; you shall leave it there until they begin to sour, for they will then be sufficiently fermented to distill them.

### Deliquescence.

I have spoken previously of Deliquescence <note>Original: "Defuillance." In an alchemical context, this refers to the process where a solid substance absorbs enough atmospheric moisture to dissolve into a liquid.</note> in the article on the Vapor Bath; it is for calcined <term>Calcination: The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the air to reduce it to a powder or "calx."</term> and moistened things that dissolve in the cold; these fall away by deliquescence, and also for those things that are squeezed under a press.

### Reverberation.

REVERBERATION is like calcination, except that reverberation is performed in a closed vessel, while calcination is done over an open fire. One uses this operation for two purposes: one, so that the spirits <note>In alchemy, "spirits" are the volatile essences or gaseous components released from a substance when heated.</note> are calcined along with the bodies; and the other, so that the thing one wishes to calcine may have more strength and be more perfect.

### Precipitation.

PRECIPITATION is an operation used after one has performed a dissolution of some metal by *aqua fortis* <term>Aqua fortis: Latin for "strong water," referring to nitric acid, a powerful solvent used to dissolve metals.</term>, and it is reduced to a powder <note>Original: "chaux" (calx). This refers to the metallic oxide or residue left after a metal has been dissolved or burned.</note>. One uses salt water <note>Original: "eau marinée," or brine.</note> to

# Torrefaction <note>The process of roasting or drying a substance over a fire to remove moisture or alter its properties.</note>

WHEN it is necessary to torrefy a compound <note>original: "un mixte." In early chemistry, this refers to a substance composed of various elements or ingredients.</note>, it is because it contains impurities that one wishes to correct by dissipating the superfluous or dangerous moisture in which it abounds. One proceeds thus: Reduce the materials you wish to torrefy—or rather, to purify and dry—into a powder if they can be crushed, or cut them into slices, and place them in a clean vessel, that is to say, one made of **tin**, because it cannot withstand violent heat, and a very gentle heat is required <note>The use of a tin vessel acted as a safety measure; if the heat became too intense, the vessel itself would melt, thus forcing the chemist to maintain the "very gentle" temperature necessary for the medicine.</note>; you will place it on a **brazier** <note>original: "rechaud," a portable stove or heater used in laboratories.</note> and stir constantly until your materials no longer give off smoke: take care to avoid this smoke, for it is dangerous. For **Opium**, **Hellebore**, **Scammony**, **Antimony**, and several others, they are prepared in different ways. For some, the flame of **Sulfur** is used to torrefy, by placing the drugs in small squares of paper which are passed over [the flame] until they no longer smoke: some carry them

->B iij<- <note>A quire signature used by early printers to help binders assemble the book in the correct order.</note>

*Decrepitation.* <note>The process of heating a salt until it stops crackling, which removes water trapped within the crystals.</note>

THIS operation has only one object, which is Salt, and is only practiced to purify it. In all sorts of Salt, there are fiery spirits <note>original: "esprits fougueux." In early chemistry, "spirits" were volatile components. Here, the author interprets the physical crackling of salt as the violent struggle of these trapped elements to escape.</note> which cannot be subdued; they flare up and make a very loud noise when they are enclosed, breaking everything that opposes their violence; but Chemistry avoids all these accidents by purifying Salts of their wicked qualities. This operation is performed in an earthenware vessel <note>original: "vaisseau de terre." These were ceramic pots designed to withstand high heat without cracking.</note> that resists fire, into which you will put the Salt you wish to decrepitate; then place the pot on glowing coals, covered with something heavy enough to resist [the internal pressure]. A great noise will occur; when it has subsided, let your Salt cool, and it will be decrepitated and prepared.

*Feces.* <note>In the context of early chemistry, "feces" (from the Latin *faex*) refers to the dregs, sediment, or solid residue remaining at the bottom of a vessel after a process like distillation or filtration.</note>

*Dead Head.* <note>original: "Teste morte." More commonly known by the Latin term "Caput Mortuum."</note>

THE dead head is a thing that is good for nothing, insipid, without taste or flavor, from which one can extract nothing; this is why it is called dead head, damned earth, or condemned earth. <note>original: "terre damnée ou condamnée." This refers to the final, exhausted dregs of a substance after all "spirits," oils, and salts have been removed by fire.</note>

### Of the Vessels.

one needs two retorts, or indeed one retort and a large Receiver called a Balloon. <note>original: "Balon." A large, spherical glass receiver used to collect and condense vapors.</note>
   To sublime, one needs a Matras or cucurbit with its capital <note>The "capital" or "head" is the top part of the still.</note> pierced at the top, or an Aludel. <note>An aludel is a pear-shaped ceramic jar, often stacked in series, used to collect condensed solids—known as sublimates—from vapor.</note>

   To reverberate, one needs two crucibles, or two pots well-luted one on top of the other. <note>original: "bien lutez." "Reverberating" refers to a furnace or setup where the flame is reflected or "reverberated" back onto the material.</note>
   To decrepitate, one needs a covered pot. <note>Decrepitation is the crackling sound made by certain salts, like common salt, when heated until their internal moisture causes them to shatter.</note>
   To torrefy, a tin vessel. <note>original: "torrifier." To torrefy is to roast or parch a substance over a fire to remove moisture or change its chemical state.</note>
   It is also necessary to have basins, pitchers, and vials. The quantity of vessels does not make the skill—

## CHAPTER VII.

### Concerning Fires.

THERE are several kinds of fires; there
are those that are natural and those that are artificial. The
first is
The fire of the Sun, to which one exposes
things that are easy to dissolve or resolve. <note>In early chemistry, to 'resolve' a substance meant to reduce it to its constituent parts.</note>
One must observe in this fire that the ves-
sels are never full, because they
break. <note>Liquids and gases expand when heated by the sun; without headspace, the pressure would shatter the glass.</note>
The fire of pigeon dung, which serves
for digestions and putrefactions, <note>Putrefaction was a controlled process of decomposition or fermentation used to 'open' a substance before further treatment.</note>
must be excited by horse dung.
The fire of horse dung serves for
the same things: it must be re-
newed every three days.
The lamp fire is the steady fire. <note>A lamp fire, often using an oil wick, provided a constant, moderate heat that did not fluctuate like a coal fire.</note>
The coal fire, for distilla-
tions by the <term>retort<note>A retort is a glass vessel with a long neck pointing downward, used for distilling substances that require higher heat.</note></term>, and the wood fire
for the cooling <term>alembic<note>A 'refrigeratory' alembic is a still equipped with a basin of cold water to condense the vapors back into liquid.</note></term>.
It is absolutely necessary to know—

## CHAPTER VIII.
### Concerning Furnaces.

**T**he use of furnaces is not permitted to every kind of person for certain specific reasons, <note>The author likely refers to the high cost of equipment, the risk of fire in crowded urban dwellings, or the legal restrictions placed on "alchemical" activities which could be mistaken for counterfeiting or sorcery.</note> which is the reason why many are deprived of performing chemical operations, persuading themselves that one cannot work without furnaces. To disabuse them of this error and to make things easier for them, I say that furnaces are not absolutely necessary, since one can perform all operations on a tripod surrounded by bricks, or on a <term>brazier<note>original: "réchaud" — a small, portable stove or metal grate used to hold hot coals.</note></term>, or in the corner of the fireplace; it is quite true that one consumes a little more wood and charcoal. Those who

Charitable & Easy. 37

will be resourceful, and who have the means to have furnaces, will be able to build them according to their desire; since the proportions are not fixed, each person makes them at their own will. <note>In the 17th century, chemical equipment was not yet standardized; practitioners often designed their own furnaces to fit specific rooms or to accommodate the particular size of their glass vessels.</note>

The materials for making furnaces are potter's clay and sand; the clay must be cut into small pieces, then sprinkled with water and left to soak little by little; when it has reached the consistency of a soft paste, one must mix in, at several intervals, three parts of sand that has been <term>sifted<note>original: "sacé" — likely a variation of "sassé," meaning passed through a sieve or "sas" to remove large pebbles.</note></term>; the whole must be kneaded together until the sand is no longer visible and the paste does not stick to the hands at all; at that point, one will be able to make the said furnaces. When they are made, they must be left to dry gently in the shade, and then put to fire in a potter's kiln if possible; failing that, cover them with embers or surround them with <term>tanner’s bark cakes<note>original: "mottes à Tanneur" — blocks of spent oak bark used in the leather tanning process, which were recycled as a slow-burning, low-intensity fuel.</note></term>, to which fire will be applied.

Some, instead of sand, use butter pots reduced to powder; although there is some effort in making it, it is also better than sand. <note>Crushing old pottery to mix into new clay is a technique known as adding "grog." This helps the furnace withstand the "thermal shock" of repeated heating and cooling without cracking.</note> One must mix

### CHAPTER IX.

*On Chemical Characters.*

The <term>Philosophers<note>In the 17th century, the term "Philosophers" often referred specifically to alchemists or "philosophers by fire" who studied the hidden properties of matter.</note></term> have done everything in their power to avoid making their operations common knowledge. They have hidden the names of the materials, the operations, and the vessels under certain <term>characters<note>original: "caracteres" — These are the symbolic glyphs and shorthand used in alchemical manuscripts to represent substances like sulfur, mercury, or vitriol, as well as equipment like the alembic.</note></term>, which has been the reason why many secrets have not been put into practice. This is why I have wished to explain them for the benefit of those who will read this book, to facilitate all sorts of operations for them, and to excuse them from having to seek their explanations elsewhere.

<note>[Circle with cross and letters 'at'] Atrament or Reddened Vitriol. <note>original: "Atrament ou Vitriol rougi" — Atrament was a general term for dark, ink-like substances or vitriols; "reddened" refers to iron sulfate that has been calcined or heated until it turns red.</note></note>
<note>[Inverted triangle with horizontal line] Azure. <note>original: "Azur" — typically referring to lapis lazuli or a blue pigment made from copper.</note></note>

<note>[Two '3' shapes joined] Cinnabar. <note>original: "Cinabre" — mercury sulfide, the primary ore of mercury and a bright red pigment.</note></note>

<note>[Inverted triangle with letter 'R'] Aqua Regia. <note>original: "Eau Royale" — "Royal Water," a mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids, so named because it is the only substance capable of dissolving the "king of metals," gold.</note></note>

<note>[A circle with a horizontal wavy line] Sea Salt. <note>original: "Sel marin" — Common table salt (sodium chloride).</note></note>
<note>[A capital 'T'] Tartar. <note>original: "Tartre" — Potassium bitartrate, the hard crust that forms on the inside of wine barrels during fermentation.</note></note>
<note>[A shape resembling a 'V' with a small 'o' or dot] Tutty. <note>original: "Tutie" — Crude zinc oxide that collects as a crust in the chimneys of furnaces where brass or zinc is smelted.</note></note>
<note>[A capital 'T' with a horizontal line through the stem] Talc. <note>original: "Talc" — Used by alchemists both as a mineral and as a base for cosmetic preparations.</note></note>
<note>[A circle with a horizontal bar and the letter 'S'] Soda. <note>original: "Soude" — Sodium carbonate, traditionally obtained from the ashes of plants like glasswort.</note></note>

<note>[A capital 'V'] Urine. <note>original: "Vrine" — Collected and distilled by alchemists as a source of phosphorus and various salts.</note></note>

<note>[A symbol resembling a capital 'V' with a small 's' or wave above it] Spirit of wine. <note>original: "Esprit de vin" — Highly concentrated ethanol, obtained through repeated distillation of wine; a primary solvent in alchemy.</note></note>
<note>[A symbol of a circle with a descending vertical line] Spirit in general. <note>original: "Esprit en general" — Volatile substances believed to be the "soul" or active principle of a material.</note></note>
<note>[A symbol resembling two crossed 'X's or a stylized 'V'] Verdigris. <note>original: "Vert de gris" — The green pigment or patina formed by the action of acetic acid on copper, used both as a pigment and a medicinal ingredient.</note></note>
<note>[A circle with a dot in the center, sometimes surrounded by rays] Wheel fire. <note>original: "Feu de roüe" — A technique where a crucible is surrounded by glowing coals to provide intense, even heat from all sides.</note></note>
<note>[A symbol consisting of two interlocking circles or a stylized '8'] Rock Salt. <note>original: "Sel Gemme" — Halite or naturally occurring mineral salt (sodium chloride), often used to increase the boiling point of liquids.</note></note>
<note>[A symbol resembling a triangle with a cross below it] Alkali Salt. <note>original: "Sel Alkali" — Potash or soda ash, derived from plant ashes; essential for making glass and soaps.</note></note>
<note>[A triangle with a cross below it and a small 's' nearby] Sulfur of the Philosophers. <note>original: "Soufre des Philosophes" — In alchemical theory, this refers not to common sulfur, but to the masculine, active, and combustible principle within all matter.</note></note>
<note>[A symbol resembling a small bucket or a 'Y' shape] Crucible. <note>original: "Creuset" — A ceramic or graphite vessel designed to withstand the extreme temperatures required for melting metals.</note></note>
<note>[A symbol of a dome with a beak or spout] Alembic. <note>original: "Alambic" — The cap or "head" of a distilling apparatus, used to catch and condense vapors.</note></note>
<note>[A symbol of a stylized 'C' or a square with internal lines] Camphor. <note>original: "Camphre" — A fragrant, white, waxy substance used for its medicinal properties and its ability to sublimate easily.</note></note>
<note>[A symbol of a thin vertical line with a flame-like top] Wick. <note>original: "Meche" — Used in oil lamps or furnaces to maintain a steady, small flame for gentle heating.</note></note>
<note>[A symbol of a stylized skull or a circle with a cross inside] Death, or dead head. <note>original: "Mort, ou teste morte" — Also known as *Caput Mortuum*, this refers to the worthless residue or "dregs" left behind after a distillation or sublimation process.</note></note>
<note>[The symbol '℞'] Signifies, Take. <note>original: "Signifie, Prenez" — Derived from the Latin *Recipe*, this symbol is still used today at the start of medical prescriptions.</note></note>
<note>[A small dot or circle] Grain weight. <note>original: "Grain pesant" — The smallest unit in the apothecaries' weight system, roughly equivalent to the weight of a single cereal grain.</note></note>
<note>[The symbol 'ß'] Half a Scruple. <note>original: ".β. Demy Scrupule" — A measurement of weight equivalent to 10 grains.</note></note>
<note>[The symbol '℈'] One Scruple. <note>original: ".I. Vn Scrupule" — An apothecary weight equal to 20 grains.</note></note>
<note>[The symbol 'ʒ' followed by 'ß'] Half a Dram. <note>original: ".β. Demi Drachme" — A weight measurement equivalent to 30 grains.</note></note>
<note>[The symbol 'ʒ'] One Dram. <note>original: ".I. Vne Drachme" — An apothecary weight equal to 3 scruples or 60 grains.</note></note>
<note>[The symbol '℥' followed by 'ß'] Half an Ounce. <note>original: ".β. Demi-Once" — Equivalent to 4 drams.</note></note>
<note>[The symbol '℥'] One Ounce. <note>original: ".I. Vne Once" — In the apothecary system, this is equal to 8 drams or 480 grains.</note></note>
<note>[The symbol 'lb'] One Pound. <note>original: ".I. Vne Livre" — The troy pound used by apothecaries, consisting of 12 ounces.</note></note>
**Ana** Equal quantities. <note>original: "Quantité égale" — A term used in prescriptions to indicate that the same amount of each preceding ingredient should be used.</note>
**Q. S.** Sufficient quantity. <note>original: "Suffisante quantité" — An instruction to add as much of a substance as is needed to achieve a desired consistency.</note>
**M.** Handful. <note>original: "Manipule" — A rough measurement based on what can be held in one hand.</note>
**P.** Pinch. <note>original: "Pugille" — A small amount that can be picked up between the thumb and two fingers.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the glossary of alchemical signs, focusing on laboratory equipment, the "Caput Mortuum," and a detailed list of apothecary weights and measures used for preparing chemical remedies.</summary>

Charitable & easy. 43

Since the observation of weights is necessary in Medicine, I wished to insert them at the end of this Part. Here is their explanation.

The <term>grain</term> <note>The smallest unit in the apothecary system, historically based on the weight of a single grain of barley.</note> is worth the weight of one large grain of barley.
The half <term>Scruple</term> <note>original: "Scrupule"</note> is worth ten grains.
The Scruple is worth twenty grains.
The half <term>Dram</term> <note>original: "Drachme"</note> is thirty grains.
The Dram is sixty grains.
The half <term>Ounce</term> <note>original: "Once"</note> is four Drams.
The Ounce is eight Drams.
The medicinal <term>pound</term> <note>original: "livre de Medecine" — Unlike the standard commercial pound, the apothecary or medicinal pound was typically divided into twelve ounces.</note> is twelve ounces.

<term>Ana</term> <note>original: "Ana" — From the Greek *ana*, meaning "of each." It is a shorthand used in prescriptions to indicate that the same quantity should be taken for every ingredient listed before it.</note> signifies equal parts of several different drugs that enter into the composition of a remedy, which are written before this word *Ana*.
<term>Handful</term> <note>original: "Manipule" — A measure of volume rather than weight, referring to the amount of herbs or materials one can grasp in a closed hand.</note> signifies what one can contain in the hand.
<term>Pinch</term> <note>original: "Pugille" — Derived from the Latin *pugillus*, this represents a smaller volume than the handful, specifically what can be picked up using only the thumb and two fingers.</note> is what one can hold with three fingers, or a pinch.

<summary>This page defines the specific weights and volumetric measurements used in early modern pharmacy, ranging from the grain and dram to informal measures like handfuls.</summary>

->*Of Plants.*<-

If I wished to write all the advantages of the Plant <note>original: "du Vegetal" — in the 17th century, this term referred broadly to the entire kingdom of plants and flora.</note>, and all its prerogatives, I would have ample material, and I would make it known that it must be preferred over both the animal and the mineral. Genesis <note>The first book of the Bible, describing the creation of the world.</note> teaches us that the Plant was the first thing created for the delights and the service of man in his state of Grace <note>The theological state of humanity before the "Fall," or the expulsion from the Garden of Eden.</note>. It contributed to his pleasures by the embellishment of the earthly Paradise, of which it was the entire ornament; and since his disgrace, he had need of it as medicine: that which previously seemed to have been made only for his pleasure, became by his fault so neces— <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("nécessaire") at the end of the page.</note>

This is what Chemistry <note>During the 17th century, the term "Chemistry" (often spelled *Chymistry*) encompassed both laboratory science and what we now call alchemy, focusing on the purification of substances.</note> does, by performing the division of substances, and rendering medicines pure, and open <note>In alchemical terms, "opening" a substance means breaking down its physical form to release its internal medicinal or "spiritual" properties.</note>, and capable of penetrating even to the most hidden and interior part of our body. ### CHAPTER I.

*Of the Vine.*

I begin with the most perfect vegetable and the most necessary to the life of man, which is the vine. The distillation of its wine must absolutely be performed in a <term>Bain-Marie<note>original: "Bain-Marie" — a water bath used for gentle, even heating, named after Mary the Jewess, an ancient alchemist.</note></term>, using glass vessels, since wine has many spirits and abounds in <term>Vitriol<note>original: "Vitriol" — historically refers to sulfate minerals, but here signifies the potent, acidic, and transformative essence found within the wine.</note></term>; it cannot be placed in any vessel made of metal or <term>lead-glazed earth<note>original: "terre plombée" — pottery coated with a lead-based glaze, which was common but would react chemically with the acidity of the wine.</note></term> without corroding the vessel or being altered itself, so great is the sympathy <note>In early science, "sympathy" and "antipathy" were used to describe how different substances naturally attracted or repelled one another.</note> it has with metals. This was well noted by a Philosopher, who

->*Of the Spirit of Tartar.*<-

WINE produces a Tartar, or Salt, which attaches itself to the walls of the vessels in which the wine is placed. The Tartar from white wine is the best. Those who have treated the virtues and perfections of the vine say that white wine is the male, and that the red is the female. I do not know if it is for this reason that white Tartar is preferred. Take some white Tartar from Montpellier, which you will crush very fine, and place into a <term>retort<note>A glass or ceramic vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids or substances under high heat.</note></term>, which you will fill at most halfway. You will adapt a large <term>receiver<note>A vessel placed at the end of the retort's neck to catch the vapors as they condense back into liquid.</note></term> to it: distill with a <term>circular fire<note>original: "feu de roüe" — a method of heating where fuel or hot coals are placed in a circle around the vessel to provide even, surrounding heat.</note></term>, and maintain the <term>degrees of fire<note>The stages of increasing heat intensity used during distillation, from gentle to very strong.</note></term>; the Spirit will come out first, followed by an oil; and when nothing more distills, let your vessels cool, and separate the oil from the Spirit using a <term>separating vessel<note>A funnel or vessel designed to separate liquids of different densities, such as oil and water.</note></term>; you will <term>cohobate<note>original: "cohoberez" — the process of pouring the distilled liquid back over the solid residue (the "feces") and re-distilling it to concentrate its medicinal properties.</note></term> the Spirit two or three times over the <term>feces<note>original: "feces" — the solid residue or dregs left at the bottom of the retort after distillation.</note></term> using an <term>ash fire<note>A gentle, indirect heat provided by burying the vessel in hot ashes.</note></term>.

This Spirit is very penetrating; it <term>incises<note>original: "incise" — in early medicine, this meant to break up or thin out thick, "viscous" bodily fluids or humors.</note></term> the humors and cleanses them; it is <term>aperitive<note>A remedy that "opens" the body's internal channels or pores to relieve obstructions.</note></term> and diuretic; the dose is of one...

### Cream or Crystal of Tartar.

TAKE Tartar, as previously described, and reduce it to a fine powder, upon which you will pour water in a glass vessel to the height of six <term>fingers<note>An informal unit of measurement based on the width of a finger, roughly 3/4 of an inch.</note></term>. Boil the said water for half an hour, and pour it by <term>inclination<note>original: "inclination" — the act of pouring off a liquid gently so as to leave the sediment at the bottom; also known as decanting.</note></term> into another glass vessel; add more water over the Tartar remaining at the bottom of the vessel, boil it, and pour it off again as previously described. Repeat this until your Tartar is completely dissolved; take all these waters and boil them until they are reduced by three-quarters. The remaining portion you shall place in a cold location; within twelve hours' time, crystals will form on the surface, which you will lift off with a silver spoon and dry—
