### *On the Essence of Sage.*
IT is a specific <term>specific: a medicine targeted toward a particular organ or disease</term> for all diseases of the brain; it stops and diverts all kinds of fluxions <note>In historical medicine, "fluxions" referred to the flow of excess humors or fluids, often causing inflammation or discharge.</note>; it causes a dead fetus <note>original: "fruit mort"</note> to be expelled and aids nature in pushing it out; the dose is from six to ten drops in a spoonful of brandy <note>original: "eau de vie"</note> or sugar water; it helps a sterile woman to conceive by taking it for eight consecutive mornings, as has been said, and abstaining during this time from the company of her husband.

### *On Sage Water.*
THIS water cleanses <term>mondifie: an archaic term for cleaning or purifying a wound or ulcer</term> wounds if they are washed with it; it blackens the hair when used as a wash; strengthens the brain and the limbs; stops blood flowing from the nose; heals stings <note>likely referring to insect stings or small puncture wounds</note>; relieves toothache; and firms the gums, by washing all the aforementioned with the said water while it is slightly warm.

### *On the Tincture or Extract of Sage.*

THIS Tincture is good against cold pains <note>In the humoral theory of medicine, "cold pains" typically referred to chronic, dull aches or rheumatic conditions associated with a lack of heat or sluggish circulation.</note> when thrown upon burning coals. The smoke, taken from below <note>Likely referring to a fumigation treatment where the patient sits over the rising smoke, a common historical method for treating gynecological conditions.</note>, stops blood loss in women. The weight of one crown <note>original: "escu"; an écu was a French coin, used here as a standard weight for the dosage, approximately 3 to 4 grams.</note> taken in pills stops vomiting. The same dose diluted in half a glass of white wine, drunk for three mornings on an empty stomach, cures quartan fever <term>quartan fever: a form of malaria characterized by a fever that recurs every fourth day (every 72 hours)</term>.

### *The Salt of Sage.*

THIS Salt should be called "Blessed" for the benefits it produces; it is universal for all kinds of diseases when taken in a vehicle <term>vehicle: an inactive substance, such as water, wine, or syrup, used to carry and administer a medicinal ingredient</term> suited to the ailment. The dose is from one scruple <note>original: "scrupule"; a traditional apothecary weight equal to 20 grains or roughly 1.3 grams.</note> up to two: it possesses the same virtues as the essence, the water, and the tincture. Recombine all four parts together <note>The author suggests combining the salt, essence, water, and tincture of Sage to create a more potent remedy.</note> and add a little deer marrow; place the whole mixture in a glass vessel, leaving a third of it empty, and seal it well; expose it for one month to the Sun, and you will have a Balm for all kinds of wounds and pains.

### On Hyssop.

THIS Vegetable is one of the most necessary to Medicine, particularly for the diseases of women; nonetheless, one finds few preparations of it made as they should be.

### On the Essence.

THE Essence is sovereign for diseases of the lung arising from cold causes <note>According to the humoral theory of the time, "cold" illnesses were often associated with an excess of phlegm or a lack of metabolic "fire," leading to congestion or sluggishness.</note>; it cures the "pale colors" <note>original: "palles couleurs"; historically referred to as "green sickness" or chlorosis, a form of anemia often attributed to young women in early modern medical texts.</note> of young women and brings about their monthly purges <note>original: "purgations"; a common historical term for menstruation.</note>; it causes the expulsion of the afterbirth <term>afterbirth: the placenta and membranes that are sometimes retained in the uterus following childbirth, which can cause infection if not removed</term> that remains after delivery; it aids respiration and gives strength; the dose is eight to ten drops taken in its own water or white wine.

### On Hyssop Water.

THIS water serves as a vehicle <note>A "vehicle" is a neutral liquid used to dilute and carry a concentrated medicine.</note> for its Essence; it is suitable for all the aforementioned diseases. The dose is from half a glass up to one glass.

### On the Tincture or Extract of Hyssop.

PILLS are made of this tincture sharpened with ten or twelve drops of its Essence. One drachm <term>drachm: a unit of apothecary weight equal to one-eighth of an ounce, roughly 3.9 grams</term> of salt of Mars <note>original: "sel de Mars"; a historical name for iron sulfate, used in medicine to treat "the vapors" or anemia.</note>, a half-drachm of epithyme <note>original: "pithyme"; a parasitic plant, also known as dodder, used in traditional medicine to treat liver and spleen ailments.</note>, one drop of essence of clove, two of cinnamon, four grains <term>grain: the smallest pharmaceutical unit of weight, approximately 65 milligrams</term> of tincture of saffron, the whole incorporated into three drachms of tincture, from which one shall make four doses. They bring back the menstrual purges <note>original: "purgations"; referring here to the menstrual cycle which, according to the medical theories of the time, "cleansed" the body of excess humors.</note> that have been stopped for a long time; by taking them in the morning, one must continue until recovery.

### On the Salt of Hyssop.

IT HAS the same virtues as its Essence, water, and tincture.

### On Tansy.

TANSY is almost unknown in France, though it has very particular qualities. The Germans and the Grisons <note>The Grisons (Graubünden) is the largest and easternmost canton of Switzerland.</note> use it as a Theriac <term>theriac: a medical concoction originally used as an antidote for poison, but later used as a general cure-all for diverse diseases.</term> against all sorts of diseases; I have experienced its effectiveness in all the following illnesses.

### On the Essence of Tansy.

IT facilitates childbirth for women, taken in doses of ten to twelve drops in two spoonfuls of cinnamon water; four drops taken in two spoonfuls of its own water kills and expels worms. The same dose taken in parsley water aids easy urination, dissolves and breaks the stone <term>stone: kidney or bladder stones, a common and painful ailment in the early modern period</term>.

### On Tansy Water.

THIS water is not at all unpleasant when Narbonne honey <note>Narbonne honey, from southern France, was famous throughout Europe for its quality and was a staple in historical pharmacies.</note> has been added to it; it expels worms from small children by giving them four or five spoonfuls to drink in the morning for three or four consecutive days during the waning of the Moon <note>original: "decours de la Lune"; many early modern medical practices were timed to lunar phases, believing the moon influenced the movement of bodily fluids and parasites.</note>. In the absence of the Essence, one may give a glass of this water to a woman in labor; it will facilitate her delivery; and if one takes a glass of this water for eight consecutive mornings before giving birth, and walks for an hour in the room, one will give birth successfully and easily.

### On Mint or Balm.

THERE ARE several kinds of Balms or Mints: red, white, cultivated, and wild; but without making distinctions between them, I will say that whatever kind they may be, their Essence is warm, and it makes the breath sweet and pleasant when five or six drops are taken in the morning on an empty stomach in a spoonful of cinnamon water; two or three drops drawn through the nose restore a lost sense of smell; its warm water strengthens cold and weakened limbs, by bathing <note>original: "estuvant"; referring to the practice of fomentation or applying warm, medicated vapors or liquids to the body</note> them a little three or four times a day for the space of fifteen days.

The Salt, the water, the Essence, and the Tincture reunited together, placed in a vial, and exposed for a month to the Sun, become a true Balm for all wounds, particularly for those of the head.

### CHAPTER III.

### *The Manner of Distilling All Kinds of Aromatic and Tender Simples.*

I have resolved, for the convenience of the reader, to restate the method for distilling aromatic Simples <note><term>Simples</term>: Individual medicinal herbs or plants used alone, rather than in a complex compound.</note> and tender Simples, as well as the manner of extracting their Tinctures and Salts. To distill aromatics, it is necessary to provide them with a Menstruum <note><term>Menstruum</term>: A solvent (such as water, wine, or spirits) used to extract the medicinal properties of a substance.</note>, especially since their Essence is found within their woody parts, and wood cannot be easily crushed; furthermore, they do not possess as much moisture as tender Simples. You shall distill them in a copper Alembic <note><term>Alembic</term>: A traditional two-part still used for distilling liquids.</note> using a Refrigeratory <note><term>Refrigeratory</term>: The cooling part of a still, typically a vessel of cold water used to condense vapors back into liquid form.</note>, as described in the first article on distillation.

<summary>This page concludes the medicinal uses of Marjoram for joint and kidney pain and introduces the formal methodology for distilling aromatic plants using solvents and copper stills.</summary>
<keywords>Marjoram, distillation, Simples, Menstruum, Alembic, Refrigeratory, pharmacy, medicinal powder</keywords>

cleanses the chest, soothes pains of the spleen, purges the fluid humors <note><term>Serosities</term>: In early medicine, these were thin, watery fluids—like serum—thought to cause illness if they accumulated in the head or joints.</note> of the head, and strengthens the limbs. It removes redness from the eyes if they are washed with it, cleans the teeth, and eases pain, and tightens the gums when gargled while slightly warm.

*Aluine, or Wormwood.*

<note>Wormwood (*Artemisia absinthium*) was highly valued in the early modern period as a "bitter" to stimulate the digestive system and the liver.</note> THE Water drawn from this herb comforts the stomach, makes the breath pleasant, excites the appetite, provokes urine, induces menstruation in young women <note>original: "purgations aux filles," a historical reference to the monthly menses.</note>, relieves those with dropsy <note><term>Hydropiques</term>: Individuals suffering from edema or excessive fluid retention.</note>, and clears obstructions of the spleen <note>original: "desopile la ratte." In humoral medicine, "deobstructing" the spleen was believed to improve mood and digestion.</note>. The dose is two to three ounces in a half-glass of white wine, drunk in the morning; this must be continued for a month.

Its Tincture has the same virtues as the water, taken in pills in the morning and one hour before supper, at a weight of one drachm <note>A <term>Drachm</term> is an apothecary weight roughly equivalent to 1/8th of an ounce or 3.9 grams.</note> each time.

## Marshmallows

THE WATER made from the leaves and roots of this herb, taken in the weight of one ounce with one ounce of sweet almond oil and drunk in the morning for the duration of a month, soothes the burning of the urine and expels the sand <note>original: "sable." Historically, "sand" or "gravel" referred to small urinary stones or sediment in the bladder.</note> found in the bladder. Its leaves, cooked in a strong wine with an ounce of honey to a slightly thick consistency and spread upon scorched tow <note>original: "filasse roussie." Tow consists of the coarse, broken fibers of flax or hemp; "scorched" or singed fibers were frequently used as an absorbent, mildly antiseptic dressing.</note>, applied to swollen and hard breasts, softens them and resolves abscesses <term>Abscess</term>: The original text uses "apostemes," an archaic term for any inflammatory swelling or localized collection of pus.. It must be applied three or four times a day, and the treatment continued.

## Pellitory-of-the-Wall

THE WATER of Pellitory-of-the-Wall <note>original: "Parietaire." *Parietaria officinalis*, a plant commonly found growing on old walls, known in herbalism for its diuretic and cooling properties.</note> is refreshing. When drunk in the weight of two to three ounces, it releases suppressed urine, heals the sores <note>original: "échauffures." These are "heat" rashes or inflammatory sores, such as canker sores or gum irritations.</note> that occur in the mouth, and soothes toothache if held in the mouth while slightly warm.

## Plantain

<note>original: "Plantain." Most likely *Plantago major* (Greater Plantain), a common herb widely used in traditional medicine for its cooling and astringent properties.</note>

THE WATER of Plantain heals obstructions <term>Obstructions</term>: The original term "opilations" refers to blockages in the internal organs—particularly the liver and spleen—which were believed to prevent the flow of vital humors. of the liver and the spleen. It cools overheated blood, binds the bowels <note>original: "referre le ventre." To tighten or constrict the digestive tract, serving as a remedy for diarrhea.</note>, protects against hemorrhoids, heals ulcers of the lungs, stops the coughing up of blood, and drives away tertian fevers <note>original: "fiévures tierces." A fever, common in malaria, where the symptoms recur every third day (approximately every 48 hours).</note> by drinking four to six ounces for three or four mornings. It heals and dries fresh wounds when they are washed three times a day with said water; it soothes the pains of burns by placing a cloth soaked in it upon the burned part, which must be frequently renewed.

Its Tincture, at the consistency of an extract and animated by its Salt <note>In Spagyric (alchemical) medicine, "animating" a tincture with its salt involved reuniting the plant's mineral salts—extracted from its burnt ashes—with its liquid essence to restore its full medicinal "life force."</note>, heals fresh wounds in five days, provided the wound is no deeper than half a finger.

## Parsley

<note>original: "Perfil." *Petroselinum crispum*, commonly used in this period as a potent diuretic.</note>

In very small, diluted doses, it was used to provide acidity to a remedy, believed to help dissolve stones and aid digestion.</term> shall be placed. ## Houseleek

<note>original: "Ioubarbe." *Sempervivum tectorum*. Its name "Jupiter's Beard" (Jovis barba) refers to its traditional use as a protection against lightning when grown on roofs.</note>

THIS water is very cold <note>In the Galenic system of humors, Houseleek was classified as having a "cold" temperament, making it the ideal treatment for "hot" diseases like fevers and inflammations.</note>: it dampens the burning heat of high fevers, heals ulcers of the intestines, soothes headaches, and stops all types of flux <term>Flux</term>: Any excessive flow of bodily fluids, most commonly referring to diarrhea (dysentery) or hemorrhaging.</term>, when taken at a weight of four to five ounces.

Its tincture repels hot abscesses <term>Apostemes</term>: An archaic term for an inflammatory swelling or abscess, often filled with pus.</term>, soothes the pains of gout arising from heat, and when mixed with oil of roses <note>original: "huile rofat." A common medicinal oil made by infusing rose petals in olive oil, used for its cooling and soothing properties.</note>, it heals burns.

# CHAPTER V

## Method for distilling the Flowers of Simples <term>Simples</term>: A term used in early medicine for a single plant or herb used on its own for healing, as opposed to a "compound" medicine made of many ingredients.</term>

### Of Roses

THERE ARE several kinds of Roses: red, pink <note>original: "incarnates." A flesh-colored or deep pink variety of rose.</note>, white, and wild roses, called Dog Roses <note>original: "Roses de chien." *Rosa canina*, a wild briar rose commonly used in historical pharmacy for its cooling properties.</note>; all of which have their own particular properties. The method of distilling them is common: everyone uses it as they please; for my part, here is my method.

Take as many Roses as you wish and clean them of all kinds of impurities; pound them in a marble or stone mortar and place them in a well-sealed glass vessel to ferment for eight or ten days in the cellar, until they begin to turn sour. Then, squeeze the said Roses under a press in a bag of very strong linen; filter the resulting liquid and distill it in a water bath <term>Bain</term>: Short for *bain-marie*. This method of indirect heating—placing the vessel in a pot of hot water—was used to gently extract the essence of delicate flowers without burning them.</term>, and seal <note>original: "luttez." To "lute" meant to hermetically seal the joints of the distillation apparatus with a paste (lute) to prevent the medicinal vapors from escaping.</note> all the [apparatus...]

### On the Tincture of Roses.

[Large initial C] That which remained at the bottom of your vessel after your distillations is a Tincture, which is purgative <note>A substance used to cleanse the bowels; at the time, this was considered a primary way to remove "corrupt humors" from the body.</note>. Another term for sulfuric acid or metallic sulfates, used here to heighten the color and acidity of the tincture.</note>; cover your vessel, and set it to infuse on hot ashes; in four hours you will have a very red Tincture, which you will filter through blotting paper,

*Conserve of Roses.*

TAKE Roses of Provins <note>The *Rosa gallica* 'Officinalis', or the Apothecary's Rose, famously cultivated in Provins, France, for its medicinal properties and lasting scent.</note>, the buds half-open: cut off the nails <note>original: "ongles." To seal a vessel with lute—a paste made of clay, lime, or other materials—to make it airtight.</note>, which you will allow to dry; then place your vessels in sand in the cellar, and leave them there for forty days: after which you will remove your vessels and place your Conserve into suitable jars to keep it.

## Violet Clairette Water

TAKE spirit of wine <term>spirit of wine|High-proof alcohol, similar to brandy or rectified ethanol, used as a solvent to extract the medicinal properties of plants.</term>, in which you will infuse violet flowers that have been well-trimmed <note>original: "épluchées." This refers to removing the green sepals and stems, leaving only the petals.</note> and cleaned, in a glass vessel. Let the spirit of wine float four fingers’ breadth above the flowers. Infuse this in a [water] bath until the flowers turn white; your spirit will then be red. You will filter this through a hippocras bag <term>hippocras bag|A conical cloth filter, often made of wool or linen, named after the Greek physician Hippocrates; it was the standard tool for straining spiced wines and cordials.</term>, and pour it back over fresh flowers, as described above. You will also add a small linen sachet containing crushed cinnamon. Upon this second infusion, your spirit will turn a violet color; you must filter it again as you did before. Dissolve sugar to your taste in rose water, then add it to your spirit, and expose the mixture to the sun for as long as possible. This water has the virtue of comforting the stomach, helping to expel phlegm, and soothing a cough. The dose is one spoonful.

<summary>This page provides a detailed recipe for Violet Clairette Water, a medicinal cordial made by double-infusing violet petals in alcohol with cinnamon and rose water. The resulting liqueur is intended to aid digestion and treat respiratory issues like coughs and phlegm.</summary>
<keywords>Violet Clairette Water, spirit of wine, infusion, hippocras bag, cinnamon, rose water, stomach tonic, expectorant, cough remedy, solar digestion</keywords>

### Water Lily Flowers
The water of water lily flowers refreshes; it induces sleep; it is good for the heat of a fever when two spoonfuls are mixed into a glass of tisane <term>tisane|A medicinal herbal infusion or barley water used as a gentle base for other remedies.</term>. It stops dysentery and the whites <note>original: "les fleurs blanches." A historical term for leukorrhea, a medical condition involving vaginal discharge, often treated in this period with cooling or astringent herbs.</note> when drunk in the weight of four ounces for three mornings in a row. It soothes gout that arises from hot causes <note>In Galenic medicine, illnesses were classified by "temperament." A "hot cause" refers to inflammation or an excess of the blood humor.</note> and relieves headaches.

Its tincture, reduced to the consistency of an extract and mixed with wax, makes a plaster for inflammations, cools the [affected] parts, and repels humors <note>original: "repercute les humeurs." Repercussives were topically applied medicines intended to drive back or disperse localized swelling or fluid buildup.</note>.

Honey is made from its flowers for refreshing enemas <note>original: "lavemens."</note>, which is prepared in the same manner as honey of violets <note>original: "le violat," a common medicinal syrup or honey infused with violet petals.</note>.

Those made from borage and bugloss flowers are better than

They are cordial <term>cordial|A medicine believed to stimulate the heart and lift the spirits.</term>, stomachic <term>stomachic|A remedy that aids digestion and strengthens the stomach.</term>, and keep the body in good condition. They purify the blood, open the appetite, induce sweating, and dissipate bile when one drinks a glass once or twice a week.

### Peach Blossoms

The distilled water of peach flowers purges as much as its syrup; but it must be taken in a larger quantity. 

The dose is three to four ounces. One drachm of its tincture, at the consistency of an extract, purges bile and serosities <note>original: "ſeroſitez." In the medical theory of the time, these were thin, watery humors believed to cause various ailments if they accumulated.</note>. If one burns its wood and leaves to make its Salt <note>This refers to the spagyric process of calcination, where a plant is burned to ash and its mineral salts are extracted to be reunited with the distilled essence, supposedly increasing its potency.</note>, adding half an ounce of the said Salt to a pint of the distilled water will make it more purgative.

### Red Poppy Flowers

While this literally translates to "leaves," in early modern botanical French, it frequently refers to the petals of a flower.</note> of your poppies, which you will crush and distill in a water bath <note>original: "au Bain." Short for "Bain-marie," a technique using a vessel placed in boiling water to provide gentle, even heat for distillation.</note> like the previous ones. This water soothes pain,

### Flowers of Great Mullein

ONE must crush the flowers of Great Mullein <note>original: "Bouillon-blanc," a plant known for its velvety leaves and yellow flowers, long used in herbal medicine for respiratory and inflammatory issues.</note> and soak them in white wine, which should cover them by the height of three fingers <note>A traditional measurement where the liquid is poured until it sits about two to three inches above the solid ingredients.</note>, and let them ferment in the cellar for three weeks; then they must be pressed, filtered, and distilled in a bain-marie <term>Bain|Short for "bain-marie," a water bath used to provide gentle, even heat for distillation.</term>. ### Orange Flowers

YOU shall put your Orange flowers, without any other preparation, into a glass gourd <term>Gourd|Also known as a cucurbit, this is the lower vessel of a distillation apparatus which holds the substance to be heated.</term>, and press them down a little so that they do not take up so much space. Lute <term>Lute|To seal the joints of the distillation apparatus with a paste—often made of clay, lime, or flour—to prevent medicinal "spirits" or vapors from escaping.</term> the head and the receiver well, for fear that the spirits may be lost; distill in a boiling water bath <term>Bain|Short for "bain-marie," a method of heating a vessel by placing it in boiling water to ensure a gentle and even temperature.</term> until nothing more rises. Extract the Salt from the mass <note>After distillation, the solid residue ("the mass") was often burned to ash and leached to extract its "essential salt," which was then added back to the distilled water to "revitalize" it.</note>, and revitalize your water, and expose it to the sun, stoppered with a piece of pierced parchment, as has been previously described.

This water stops the suffocations that arise from the womb <note>original: "suffocations qui proviennent de la matrice." Historically, this referred to a condition where the uterus was believed to move within the body, causing a sensation of choking or anxiety, often called "hysterical suffocation."</note>. One may take one to three ounces of it at the onset of the fit. Moreover, it induces vomiting without effort.

->*Of Fruit Waters, and the manner of*<-

*Reinette Apples.*

IT is only right to begin this Chapter with the Queen of fruits. It is not without reason that the Ancients gave it the name Reinette <note>The author is playing on the French word "Reine" (Queen), suggesting that the "Reinette" apple is a "little queen" among fruits.</note>. It possesses very particular virtues for the maintenance of our health, and it contributes to its restoration. You shall cut your apples into very thin slices, removing the seeds, and place them layer upon layer in a glass Cucurbit <term>Cucurbit|The bottom part of a distilling apparatus, typically shaped like a gourd, which holds the substance to be heated.</term>, which you shall fill at most two-thirds full. Lute <term>Lute|To seal the joints of the distillation equipment with a specialized paste or clay to prevent medicinal vapors from escaping.</term> the Cap <note>Original: "Cape." This refers to the "head" or alembic top that sits on the cucurbit.</note> and the Receiver, and distill in a boiling bath <note>A "Bain-marie" or water bath, where the vessel is heated by boiling water rather than a direct flame to ensure a gentle, even temperature.</note>, and continue until nothing more rises. This water is pleasant: it refreshes and moistens; it is a friend to the lungs; it preserves a healthy plumpness <note>Original: "embon-point." In the 17th century, a well-rounded physique was considered a sign of vigor and good health.</note>; it induces sleep, quenches thirst, and keeps the body in good [order]...

## *Strawberries.*

**S**trawberries are distilled in two manners: in the Sun, and in a Water Bath <term>Bain|Also known as a Bain-Marie; a method of heating a substance by placing its container in boiling water to ensure gentle, even heat.</term>. For both operations, one must crush the fruit and set it to ferment in the cellar. Those you wish to distill in the Sun, you shall place in vessels, as is described in the Article on the Syrup of Violets <note>Original: "Syrop violat." A syrup made from violet flowers, often used for its cooling and soothing properties.</note> drawn without fire.

This water is good for preserving the complexion, and for removing spots from the face.

The other manner of distilling is in a Water Bath. This water is good against all kinds of poisons. It promotes purgations <note>In 17th-century medicine, "purgations" referred to the healthy regulation and "cleansing" of bodily fluids, including menstruation or bowel movements.</note> and strengthens the stomach, if one drinks half a glass of it in the morning. If one washes watery eyes with this water, after having been purged, it dries them and stops the discharge <note>Original: "fluxion." A medical term for an abnormal flow of fluid or inflammation toward a specific part of the body.</note>.

place it to digest in a water bath until your water is tinted. Pour off your tincture by decantation <note>Original: *par inclination*; the act of tilting the vessel to pour off the liquid while leaving the sediment undisturbed.</note>, and put fresh water over what remains in your vessel. Let it infuse as before, and continue until your water is no longer tinted. If you wish to form pills from it, you will let it evaporate to the consistency of an extract. 

Although tinctures are purer than the things from which they are extracted, they must nevertheless be taken in the same weight as before their purification; for the more remedies are purified, the less violent they are <note>Meurdrac highlights a key principle of her chemistry: that refining a substance removes its "violent" or harsh qualities, allowing the patient to take a full dose of the pure essence without the side effects of the raw material.</note>.

It is a specific remedy for the liver. It cures jaundice and strengthens after having purged, which is why one...

*Tincture of Senna.*

## Tincture of Black Hellebore.

ONE uses spirit of wine <term>Spirit of wine|A high-proof distilled alcohol used as a solvent to extract the "essence" or active medicinal properties of a substance.</term> to extract Tinctures from complex substances <note>Original: *Mixtes*; refers to natural compounds or materials where the medicinal properties are difficult to separate.</note> that are difficult to penetrate. Hellebore requires a penetrating menstruum <term>Menstruum|A solvent, such as alcohol or vinegar, used in pharmacy to dissolve and extract nutrients or medicines.</term>; this is why the spirit must be of high quality. You shall cut the wood into small pieces and proceed as you have done above. Its virtues are to purge all melancholic humors <note>In humoral medicine, "black bile" or melancholy was one of the four fluids believed to regulate health; an excess was thought to cause both physical and mental ailments.</note>, and consequently all the diseases that arise from them, such as mania, madness, hypochondriacal passions <note>A historical diagnosis for distress—often involving anxiety or digestive issues—believed to stem from the upper abdominal organs.</note>, vertigo, cancers, quartan fever <note>A form of malaria where the fever returns every four days (seventy-two-hour intervals).</note>, apoplexy, epilepsy, scabs and black itches, and other diseases of the same kind. But one must consider the strength of the patients before using it. It is advisable to consult a prudent Physician, as the remedy is violent <note>In early pharmacy, "violent" indicated a very powerful or harsh substance that could be toxic in the wrong dosage.</note>.

## Tincture of Gamboge.
<note>Gamboge (*Gomme-gutte*) is a resin collected from several species of evergreen trees of the genus *Garcinia*. In early modern medicine, it was used as a powerful, often harsh, purgative.</note>

It has the power to expel serous fluids <term>Serous fluids|A watery discharge from the blood; in the humoral system, excess "serosity" was thought to cause swelling or edema.</term> through both the upper and lower channels <note>That is, acting as both an emetic to induce vomiting and a purgative to move the bowels.</note>, drawing them from the entire body, as well as viscous and putrid humors. This is why it is used for dropsy <term>Dropsy|Now known as edema; a condition characterized by the accumulation of fluid in the body's tissues or cavities.</term>, and for long-lasting fevers, the itch <note>The original French *galles* and *gratelles* refer to scabies and various itchy skin eruptions or rashes.</note>, and rashes. The dose is from six grains to fifteen <term>Grains|A traditional unit of weight used by apothecaries; approximately 0.06 grams.</term>.

### Of Nutmeg.

NUTMEG is not distilled at all; its spirits are condensed together and have no ease for rising <note>In early chemistry, "spirits" were the volatile components of a substance. The author explains that nutmeg's oils are too heavy to easily vaporize during standard distillation.</note>. What is called essence of Nutmeg is rather a tincture than an essence. It is extracted in this manner:

Cut your Nutmeg into slices and place it in a glass cucurbit <note>A cucurbit is the gourd-shaped lower vessel of a distillation apparatus.</note>, and pour spirit of wine <note>original: "esprit de vin" — a high-proof alcohol similar to modern brandy or ethanol used as a solvent.</note> over it; then put on a blind head <note>original: "Chapiteau aveugle" — a still-head without a spout or beak, designed to let vapors rise and fall back into the vessel (reflux) rather than being collected.</note>, and let it digest in a Water Bath <note>original: "Bain" — referring to a bain-marie, which provides gentle, indirect heat.</note> over a lukewarm fire until your spirit is colored.

Then pour it off by inclination <note>The process of decanting: carefully pouring off the liquid while leaving the sediment undisturbed.</note>, and put more spirit over the remains <note>original: "feces" — the solid dregs or dross left at the bottom of the vessel after extraction.</note> staying at the bottom of the vessel. Repeat this until your spirit no longer draws any color <note>original: "teinture" — the coloring or extract.</note>. Take all your tinctures and recover your spirit through distillation in the Water Bath; the essence, or rather the tincture, will remain at the bottom.

CHAPTER IX.

*On Gums & Resins.*

*Turpentine.*

[Large initial T] All Gums and Resins are distilled *by descent* <term>Distillation *per descensum* (by descent) is a technique where the heat source is applied to the top or sides of the vessel, forcing the liquefied substance or vapor to travel downwards into a receiver, rather than rising as in standard distillation.</term>, as is mentioned in its proper place. From some of them, the tincture is extracted in the manner I have described regarding the extraction of Tinctures. Of all the resinous Gums, Turpentine is the only one that is distilled by a Retort <note>A retort is a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances.</note> in this manner. Take Venice Turpentine <note>Venice turpentine is a high-quality resin harvested from the European Larch tree, prized in early medicine and art for its clarity.</note>; the whitest is the best, and place it in a retort; pour water over it until it floats two fingers <note>An informal measurement roughly equal to the width of two fingers, or about 3–4 centimeters.</note> above the resin; let your vessel, at most, be filled only halfway. Attach another retort <note>In this setup, the second retort acts as a "receiver" to collect the distilled liquid.</note>, and lute <term>Luting (from the word "lut") refers to sealing the joints of the glass apparatus with a specialized paste or clay to make them airtight and prevent the escape of vapors.</term> the joints well: distil over a gentle fire, for fear that the material might swell: continue your operation until you see a red oil rise— <note>The text ends mid-word with "rou-", likely "rouge" (red), as the distillation of turpentine produces different grades of oil, often turning darker or redder as the heat continues.</note>

CHAPTER XI.

*Method of Preparing Feculas.* <note>In early chemistry, a "fecula" (from the Latin *faecula*, meaning dregs or sediment) refers to the starchy powder obtained by allowing the juice of certain plants to settle.</note>

*Bryony Feculas.* <note>Bryony (*Bryonia*) is a genus of climbing plants. Though toxic in large amounts, it was frequently used in historical medicine as a powerful purgative and treatment for fluid retention.</note>

TAKE some Bryony roots and
cut them, then crush them in a
marble mortar, and squeeze them
under a press; a thick and
white liquid will come out, which you
shall place in a bowl in the cellar for
twelve hours; all the whiteness will 
settle at the bottom of the bowl. 
Pour off the water from the top by 
inclination <note>A technique of pouring off liquid slowly so as not to disturb the sediment; known today as decanting.</note>, and dry the white 
matter with gentle heat. If your roots
are dry when you crush them,
you shall soak them in common water, 
and proceed as above. All feculas 
are prepared in this manner. The 
virtues of this one are to purge 
serous and pituiteous <term>pituiteous: relating to phlegm or mucus, one of the four traditional humors believed to regulate health.</term> humors; 
it clears obstructions of the liver, 
and of the spleen; causes the waters 
of the dropsical <note>Dropsy, or edema, is the swelling caused by the accumulation of excess fluid in body tissues.</note> to be expelled 
from above and below <note>A historical medical euphemism meaning the substance acts as both an emetic (causing vomiting) and a laxative.</note>; 
and provokes the months <note>A common early modern term for inducing or regulating menstruation.</note>.

### FOREWORD.

**L**ES Animals, as well as **Simples** <term>Simples: In traditional medicine, these are individual medicinal plants or natural substances used on their own, rather than as part of a complex compound or "polypharmacy" mixture.</term>, contribute to the maintenance of human life and to the preservation of health; and even help to restore it when it is weakened. Experience makes this known to us, since they furnish us with remedies for this purpose: If they cause us infirmities through our own poor conduct, they serve as an antidote to destroy them. ## CHAPTER ONE.

->### On Human Blood.<-

MANY have written about the manner of distilling human blood, and have made this operation extremely laborious; this has discouraged many people and prevented them from undertaking it.

136 *Chemistry*

Here is a very easy method, which has been proven by experience. Take the blood of a young man, aged between eighteen and twenty-four years, who is well-tempered <note>In historical medicine, a "well-tempered" person possessed a perfect balance of the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—signifying ideal health.</note>, with a fresh and ruddy complexion, and who is neither too fat nor too thin. Let the blood cool and rest for twelve hours; then you shall remove all the water <note>original: "l'eau"; this refers to the serum that separates from the blood clot after it sits.</note> that has risen to the top, and take only the solid mass. Cut this into small pieces, place them in a glass <term>Cucurbit: The bottom part of a distillation apparatus, usually gourd-shaped, which holds the substance to be heated.</term>, and distill them in a <term>Bain-Marie: A water bath (literally "Mary's bath") used to provide gentle, steady heat by surrounding the vessel with hot water.</term> over a low fire until dry. Dry the residue remaining at the bottom of your vessel over a small fire until it turns into a powder, and keep this powder in a well-sealed glass vial, to be used as will be described hereafter. Put fresh blood, of the same quality as the first, into your Cucurbit; pour the water you previously distilled over it, and distill just as before. Repeat this operation five times, and each time remove your powder; then set your distilled water to circulate in a water bath, or in <term>Manure: Piles of fermenting horse manure were used by early chemists as a "natural furnace" to provide a constant, gentle warmth for long-term chemical reactions.</term> for fifteen days; then distill for the last time in a water bath over a fire

<summary>This page outlines a specific spagyric method for distilling human blood, involving repeated cycles of dehydration and re-distillation to produce a medicinal powder and "circulated" water.</summary>

gentle, and draw off only two parts of the water you set to circulate; the remaining part is only phlegm <note>In early chemistry, "phlegm" referred to the watery, tasteless, and inert byproduct of distillation, which was considered medicinally useless compared to the active "spirits."</note>, which is good for nothing. This water should be called the **Spirit of Spirits**, because of its great subtlety <note>"Subtlety" refers to the fineness and purity of the substance, believed to allow it to penetrate the smallest vessels of the body.</note> and the source from which it is extracted. Its virtues surpass those attributed to **potable gold** <term>Potable Gold: Known as *Aurum Potabile*, this was a legendary alchemical panacea consisting of gold dissolved in a liquid form, believed to cure all ailments and prolong life.</term>. The dose is one to two spoonfuls, in some **cordial water** <note>A medicinal liquid, usually sweetened and infused with herbs, intended to stimulate the heart and spirits.</note> or a liquid appropriate to the ailment.

          CHAPTER III.

*On the Admirable Oil of Human Bones.*

rises; expose this liquid
to the sun. It is a specific <note>A "specific" in early modern medicine was a remedy believed to have a direct, curative effect on a particular disease or part of the body.</note> for all
pains of the nerves and joints, and
for sciatica.

### CHAPTER VIII.

#### On Wax Oil.

TAKE yellow wax, of a good scent, as much as you wish; melt it, and mix it with a fourth part of crushed brick, or very clean sand, and make small pellets of it that can fit into the Retort <term>Retort: (Historical: Cornuë) A glass or earthenware vessel with a long, downward-sloping neck used for distilling substances by heating them.</term>, which you shall fill no more than half-way. Take care not to let your wax swell up <note>Distilling wax is volatile; the addition of crushed brick or sand helps distribute the heat and prevents the wax from "bumping" or foaming over into the neck of the vessel.</note>: you shall distill this over an ash fire <note>A method of indirect heating where the vessel is placed in a bed of hot ashes to ensure a gentle, even temperature.</note>, and maintain the degrees of the fire. When no more smoke comes out, your operation will be finished. Separate the water from the oil using a separating vessel, and pour the oil back onto the dregs <note>original: "feces." In early chemistry, this refers to the solid residue left at the bottom of the vessel after distillation, not waste matter.</note>, and cohobate <term>Cohobate: The process of redistilling a liquid by pouring it back over the solid remains of the substance from which it was just drawn, intended to further concentrate or "exalt" its properties.</term> twice; you will then have a clear and clean oil. Its virtues are to resolve hardnesses <note>To soften or dissolve tumors, cysts, or inflammatory swellings.</note>; it penetrates and dissipates scars, if one continues to apply it for a long time: it heals wounds that are made by

### CHAPTER IX.

#### On Butter Oil.

TAKE fresh butter that is newly made; you shall make small pellets of it, as you did with the wax, using ashes or sand, and place them in the Retort <term>Retort: (Historical: Cornuë) A glass or earthenware vessel with a long, downward-sloping neck used for distilling substances by heating them.</term>, and you shall distill it over an ash fire <note>Indirect heating where the vessel is nestled in hot ashes for a gentle, steady temperature.</note>. Separate the water from the oil, and cohobate <term>Cohobate: The process of redistilling a liquid by pouring it back over the solid remains (dregs) of the substance from which it was just drawn, intended to concentrate its medicinal properties.</term> as described above.

This oil is excellent for all kinds of coughs and rheums <note>Rheums: A historical term for watery discharge from the nose or eyes, or mucus accumulation in the chest, typically associated with a cold or congestion.</note>. The dose is five or six drops in sugar water, or hyssop <note>Hyssop: A herb of the mint family, traditionally used in medicinal waters to treat respiratory issues.</note> water, drunk evening and morning. It soothes pains in the side <note>Likely referring to pleurisy or "stitches" in the chest.</note>, if one makes a poultice <note>original: "cataplaſme." A soft, moist mass of herbs or flour applied to the skin to treat aching or inflamed parts of the body.</note> of it with cumin flour. It must be applied three times a day.

### CHAPTER X.

#### *On the Oil of Hair.*

TAKE some Hair, and make it into small pellets <note>original: "pelottes." Small balls or rounded masses.</note>, and place them in a Retort <term>Retort: (Historical: Cornuë) A glass or earthenware vessel with a long, downward-sloping neck used for distilling substances by heating them.</term>, which you shall fill to at most three-quarters full, and fit a Receiver <term>Receiver: (Historical: Recipient) A vessel used to collect the liquid products of distillation.</term> to the Retort, which you shall lute <term>Lute: To seal the joints of the distilling apparatus with a paste to ensure it is airtight.</term>: then distill over a wheel fire <term>Wheel fire: (Historical: Feu de roüe) A method of heating where fuel is placed in a circle around the vessel, allowing for more intense heat than a simple ash bath.</term> until no more smoke comes out.

This oil has a very penetrating odor. It is good for suffocations of the womb <note>original: "suffocations de la matrice." A historical medical term for a condition once believed to be caused by the movement of the uterus, often involving difficulty breathing or fainting.</note>: one must rub the temples and the nostrils with it. It also makes hair grow, if one applies it for eight or ten days in a row on the place where one wants it to grow. Before applying it, the head must be well rubbed with a slightly warm cloth.

### CHAPTER XI.

#### On the Spirit of Milk.

BRING the milk to a boil; pour in a spoonful of vinegar, then filter the said milk, and place it in a glass Cucurbit <term>Cucurbit: (Historical: Cucurbite) The lower part of a distilling apparatus, shaped like a gourd, which holds the liquid to be heated.</term>, and distill it in a boiling water bath <term>Bain-marie: (Historical: Bain boüillant) A method of heating a vessel by placing it in boiling water to ensure a gentle, even temperature.</term>, and continue until nothing more rises. Remove the dregs <note>original: "feces." The solid residue remaining after distillation.</note> that are in the Cucurbit, and rectify your spirit <term>Rectify: The process of purifying a liquid through repeated distillation to increase its potency and clarity.</term>. Decrease your fire by one degree, and from the four pints that you had, you should only draw out three pints and a chopin <note>original: "choppine." A French unit of measurement roughly equivalent to half a pint or approximately 475 milliliters.</note>: remove the dregs, and rectify once more, and distill over a lukewarm fire, and distill only three pints.

The use of this spirit is more healthful and more refreshing than common whey <note>original: "petit-laict." The liquid remaining after milk has been curdled and strained.</note>; its coldness is corrected by the fire, and the bad qualities are separated. It is not at all harmful to the stomach: one may add rose sugar or violet sugar to it <note>syrups or sugars infused with flower essences, commonly used to make medicines more palatable</note>, according to the needs of the patient.

### FOREWORD.

**A**LTHOUGH Metals and Minerals seem to be distant from man, <note>In the early modern worldview, minerals were considered "distant" because they belonged to the lowest rung of the "Great Chain of Being," lacking the vegetative or sensitive life found in plants and animals.</note> and though Holy Scripture makes no mention of their creation, <note>The author refers to the Book of Genesis, where the creation of light, plants, and animals is detailed, but the formation of underground minerals is not explicitly described.</note> nevertheless they do not fail to provide us with very healthy remedies. Today, Medicine uses them with great success. It is necessary that their preparations be exactly made, because these are potent <note>original: "violens." In this context, it refers to the high chemical activity or toxicity of mineral-based medicines compared to gentler herbal remedies.</note> remedies; even though one only takes them in small quantities, and for stubborn and long-standing illnesses. When I began this book, I...

had proposed not to go beyond my own experiences. This is why I am omitting from this Part the operations on gold and on silver, since I am not acquainted with their preparations, nor their utility in Medicine. I have seen several operations which were given the names of <term>Potable Gold<note>original: "Or potable." A legendary alchemical elixir consisting of gold dissolved in a liquid, which was highly sought after for its supposed life-extending and curative properties.</note></term>, tincture of gold, and oil of silver, which I could not understand; for I cannot persuade myself that bodies so perfect and condensed could be liquefied. <note>In the alchemical tradition, gold and silver were considered "perfect" metals because they were resistant to fire and corrosion. Their "condensed" or dense nature made many chemists skeptical that they could be truly dissolved into a medicinal "oil" or "tincture" without losing their metallic essence.</note> It is not that I condemn these operations simply because I cannot conceive of them; I would be as rash as the blind who would assert that there is no Sun, simply because they could not see it. As for the operations that follow, I assure you that they are true and have been tested by experience.

<summary>In this passage, the author justifies omitting gold and silver from his chemical treatise, expressing skepticism toward "potable gold" while maintaining a humble stance on his own lack of knowledge regarding these "perfect" metals.</summary>
<keywords>gold, silver, potable gold, alchemy, chemical experience, medicinal preparations, epistemology</keywords>

### Of Vitriol.

TAKE five or six pounds of <term>Roman Vitriol<note>original: "Vitriol Romain." Also known as blue vitriol or copperas; this is a sulfate mineral (typically iron or copper sulfate) used as a primary ingredient in many early chemical processes.</note></term>, otherwise called **Copperas**, and place them in an earthen pot that is not glazed, and set it upon the fire, and surround it with charcoal, and leave it until the Vitriol becomes red: you shall stir it from time to time with an iron spatula. Vitriol <term>calcined<note>Calcination involves heating a substance to a high temperature, but below its melting point, to bring about thermal decomposition or to remove volatile fractions.</note></term> until it reaches redness is called by the Chemists <term>Colcotar<note>original: "Colcotar." The reddish-brown residue (iron oxide) remaining after the distillation of sulfuric acid from iron sulfate.</note></term>: take it, and grind it, and put it into a <term>Retort<note>original: "Cornuë." A vessel with a long, curving neck used for the distillation of substances, designed to withstand high heat.</note></term> made of <term>Beauvais earth<note>original: "terre de Beauvais." Beauvais was famous for its stoneware clay, which was exceptionally resistant to thermal shock, making it ideal for laboratory equipment like retorts.</note></term>; this earth resists the fire. Let it be filled to no more than two-thirds; attach a large receiver called a <term>Balloon<note>original: "Balon." A large, spherical glass vessel used to collect and condense the vapors coming from the retort.</note></term>, and <term>lute<note>original: "luttez." The process of sealing the joints between the retort and the receiver with a paste to prevent the escape of chemical vapors.</note></term> them with egg whites, <term>quicklime<note>Calcium oxide, a caustic alkaline substance that, when mixed with egg whites, creates a very strong, heat-resistant adhesive seal.</note></term>, and <term>Spanish white<note>original: "blanc d'Espagne." A pigment made from finely ground chalk or bismuth, used here as a filler in the sealing paste.</note></term>: place your Retort upon the furnace, or in the corner of a chimney,

Care must be taken that it does not touch any part other than the affected area, as it corrodes the flesh. It whitens the teeth if they are rubbed with it using a small cloth; it also helps to extract the <term>tinctures<note>A medicinal extract prepared by soaking a substance (in this case, flowers) in a solvent.</note></term> of all kinds of flowers.

The virtues of this spirit are to <term>incise<note>In early medicine, to "incise" humors meant to break up or thin thick, viscous fluids like phlegm so they could be expelled.</note></term>, <term>disperse<note>original: "discuter." ### Mineral Crystal.

Mineral Crystal is very useful in medicine, and the manner of making it is very easy. Take a <term>German crucible<note>A melting pot made of high-quality Hessian clay, known for its ability to withstand extreme heat.</note></term>, place inside one pound of Saltpeter, and set it to melt over coals under the chimney at moderate heat. When the fusion is complete, throw in—in three or four separate additions—two ounces of <term>flowers of Sulfur<note>A fine powder produced by sublimating natural sulfur; used frequently in both alchemy and medicine.</note></term>, or in their absence, sulfur crushed very small. Let it boil for a quarter <note>The text likely means a quarter of an hour; this was a common instruction in chemical recipes of the period.</note>

# CHAPTER III.

## Of Sea Salt.

### Of the spirit of Sea Salt.

This spirit is extracted in two ways; one by distillation, the other by dissolution and <term>deliquescence<note>Original: "defaillance." In early chemistry, this refers to "deliquium," the process by which a salt absorbs enough atmospheric moisture to dissolve into a liquid.</note></term>. For both, it is necessary to <term>decrepitate<note>Original: "decrepiter." To heat salt until the crackling sound—caused by the expansion of water trapped in the crystals—stops, indicating it is dried.</note></term> the salt, as was stated in the Chapter on Operations. Take your decrepitated salt and grind it very fine: if you wish to distill it, mix two pounds of salt with one pound of brick powder, or fragments of the pot in which you decrepitated it. Put the whole into a <term>retort<note>Original: "Cornuë." A bulbous glass or earthenware vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck, used to distill substances by heating.</note></term>, and attach to it a <term>receiver<note>Original: "Recipient." A vessel designed to collect the vapors condensed during the distillation process.</note></term>, in which you will put one pound of water. <term>Lute<note>Original: "Luttez." To seal the joints of the vessels with a paste (lute) to prevent the escape of volatile vapors.</note></term> the joints and distill just as you did for the spirit of Vitriol. Your operation being completed, unseal your vessels and put what is in the receiver into a glass <term>cucurbit<note>Original: "Cucurbite." A gourd-shaped vessel that forms the lower part of a still or alembic.</note></term>, and draw off the water that you put there <unclear>previously</unclear>.

### CHAPTER VI.

### On the Essence of Carabé, or Amber.

I have been in doubt as to which kingdom <note>Referring to the "Three Kingdoms of Nature": Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral. Chemists debated whether amber was a plant resin or a mineral substance.</note> I should place this operation, although **Amber** is well enough known, especially since Authors speak of it diversely. Some place it in the category of **Gums**, others among the **Minerals**, especially since it is found mixed with gold. For my part, I am of the opinion of those

-> Charitable & easy. 167 <-

...who consider it a **Bitumen** <note>A naturally occurring petroleum substance. The author uses its burning smell to argue it belongs to the mineral kingdom rather than the vegetable.</note>, because of the strong odor it gives off when it is burned. This is what obliges me to place it among the **Minerals**. Take white or yellow **Amber**, and place it in a **Retort** <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances.</note>, filled at most halfway; distill it in a **sand bath** <note>A method of heating a vessel evenly by surrounding it with sand, which allows for gradual and controlled temperature changes.</note>; and when no more smoke comes out of the Retort, cease your operation. If some water is found with the essence, you will separate it using a separatory vessel.

This oil, or essence, is very useful. It cures **apoplexy** <note>A historical term for a stroke or sudden loss of consciousness.</note>, three drops taken in a spoonful of wine, or sugar water. It promotes urine and causes the discharge of **gravel** <note>Small stones or sediment in the urinary tract.</note> when taken in parsley or fennel water. It is good for the **falling sickness** <note>A historical name for epilepsy.</note>, and for **suffocations of the womb** <note>A common diagnosis in early modern medicine (often called hysteria) where the uterus was believed to move or cause respiratory distress.</note>, and convulsions, taken in sage water. Applied externally, it heals paralytics, wounds, and ulcers, and draws out **splinters** <note>Specifically "esquilles," referring to small fragments of bone broken off from a wound.</note>. It comforts the stomach and stops **diarrhea** <note>Original: "dévoyement," meaning a loosening of the bowels.</note>, if one rubs the aforementioned parts with it.

#### CHAPTER VII.

*Of Coral.*

*Of the Tincture of Coral.*

**T**AKE the reddest Coral, and reduce it into small pieces, and put them in a **Matrass** <note>A long-necked glass flask used in chemistry for digestion and distillation.</note>: pour over it filtered lemon juice, so that it sits four finger-widths <note>A traditional unit of measurement; four fingers is roughly equivalent to 3 inches or 7.5 centimeters.</note> above the coral: seal your vessel, and set it for **digestion** <note>The process of softening or extracting substances using gentle, steady heat.</note> in a **Bain-marie** <note>A water bath used to provide uniform, moderate heat, preventing the substance from burning.</note>, and leave it there until you see your liquid turn red. Then you will separate it by **decanting** <note>Original: "inclination"; the act of pouring off the liquid while leaving the sediment at the bottom.</note>, and put more lemon juice over your remaining Coral, and set it for digestion as you did before, and continue until your juice no longer extracts any tincture. Take all the tinctures; for every **pound** <note>Original: "livre," a pre-metric unit of weight roughly equal to 489 grams.</note> of liquid, you will add two pounds of sugar, and cook it until it reaches the consistency of a syrup.

### CHAPTER VIII.

## Of Antimony.

### Of the Saffron of Antimony <note>Original: "Crocus d'Antimoine." In early chemistry, "Crocus" referred to a metal that had been calcined to a reddish or deep yellow powder, resembling the color of saffron.</note>.

The **Philosophers** <note>A term used by the author to refer to alchemists and natural scientists of previous generations.</note> have worked with **Antimony** in so many ways that one could write several volumes on their operations; I will limit myself to three or four, whose effects are certain and well-tested. Take equal parts **Antimony** and **Saltpeter** <note>Potassium nitrate, used here as an oxidizing agent to help the antimony reach the desired state.</note>; pulverize them each separately, then mix them together, and place a spoonful in a cast-iron mortar over glowing coals. You will set fire to it with a piece of charcoal, then cover the mortar with the fire shovel. And once the boiling has subsided, stir it with an iron rod, and put—

And when the matter is reddish, you will remove the mortar from the fire, and wash and sweeten <term>dulcorerez: the process of washing a precipitated or calcined substance with water to remove bitter or acrid salts</term> it five or six times with common water: for the last wash, you will add two spoonfuls of cinnamon water. <margin>This powder is called Saffron because of its color.</margin> It has the virtue <note>In historical medicine, "virtue" refers to the inherent power or medicinal property of a substance.</note> of causing gentle vomiting: it cures long and stubborn fevers; it purges through urine and sweat; sometimes through the stool. The dose is from eight to fifteen grains <note>A traditional unit of mass; 15 grains is approximately 1 gram.</note>, infused from evening until morning in a glass of white wine. One must take only the wine and leave the powder behind. This is what is called **Emetic Wine**. There is an Emetic powder made with **Antimony**, **Sublimated Mercury** <note>Also known as corrosive sublimate (mercuric chloride); it was a potent and toxic chemical used in highly controlled alchemical preparations.</note>, and **Vitriol** <note>A sulfate of a metal, such as iron or copper, often used as a reagent.</note>; which is more violent than this one.

## Diaphoretic Antimony
<note>A "diaphoretic" is a substance used to induce sweating, traditionally used to "break" a fever or expel toxins from the body through the skin.</note>

TAKE **Antimony** and **Saltpeter** <note>Also known as nitre (potassium nitrate), used here as an oxidizing agent to process the antimony.</note>, in equal parts; reduce them to a powder, and place them in a **Crucible**, which you will cover with another Crucible pierced at the base. Lute <term>luttez: the act of sealing a vessel with "lute," a paste made of clay, sand, and binders to make it airtight and heat-resistant</term> them together, and when the lute is dry, place them in the midst of burning coals; a struggle <note>original: "combat"; This refers to the violent chemical reaction—a deflagration—that occurs when saltpeter and antimony are heated together, creating noise and sparks.</note> will occur that makes a noise, as in the previous operation. After three hours, remove your Crucibles, and take your material, and reduce it to powder, and mix it with as much Saltpeter as you put in the previous time; put the whole into the luted Crucibles, and place in a hot fire for eighteen or twenty hours, and until the material is very white. Then you will grind it, wash it, and filter it three or four times, to remove the acridity of the Saltpeter. The final water in which you wash it must be some cordial water <note>A medicinal water intended to strengthen the heart or "cordial" spirits; common bases included distilled herbs or spices.</note>, such as **Rose**, **Cinnamon**, **Anise**, or **Fennel**.

This powder is sudorific <note>Causing or inducing sweat.</note>; it purges through the urine and through sweat.

H iiij <note>A printer's signature mark used to ensure the pages were bound in the correct sequence.</note>

The dose is ten to fifteen grains <note>A grain is a traditional apothecary unit of weight, roughly equivalent to 65 milligrams.</note> in rose preserve <note>A medicinal paste made from rose petals and sugar, used to mask the taste of medicines.</note>, or the pulp of cooked apples.

*Oil, or Syrup of Antimony.*

TAKE **Antimony** pulverized as you see fit; place it in a **Crucible** over the fire, and leave it there for five or six hours, stirring it constantly with an iron rod, until the **Antimony** has acquired a grayish color. Remove the **Crucible**, and let it cool: take the mass which you will reduce to powder, and mix it with an equal part of **fine sugar**. Put the whole into a **Retort** <note>A glass or ceramic vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids or extracting essences.</note>, and distill with a circular fire <note>original: "feu de roüe"; a heating technique where burning coals are arranged in a ring around the vessel, often increased in intensity as the process continues.</note> until nothing more rises. This oil purges gently through the stools, and without violence: twelve to twenty drops placed in an infusion of one dram <note>original: "un gros"; an apothecary weight equal to about 3.8 grams.</note> of **Senna** <note>A plant-based laxative still used in modern medicine.</note>, cures quartan and tertian fevers <note>Types of recurring fevers often associated with malaria; tertian fevers occur every three days, and quartan fevers every four.</note>. It must be taken three times when the fevers are stubborn, leaving an interval of one or two days, according to the strength of the patient, and the effect produced by the previous dose of the remedy.

### Tincture of Antimony.

CRUSH **Antimony** into an impalpable powder <note>A powder so fine that its individual grains cannot be felt between the fingers.</note>, and place it in a large earthenware vessel which is not glazed, and which can withstand the heat of the fire. Place it upon a furnace or brazier, and maintain a moderate fire. Stir the powder constantly with a small iron rod until the sulfur of **Antimony** <note>In early chemistry, "sulfur" referred to the combustible or oily principle within a substance rather than just the element sulfur.</note> is entirely consumed; you will know this has occurred when it no longer produces smoke or a blue flame. At least forty-eight hours <note>original: "deux fois vingt-quatre heures"; literally two times twenty-four hours.</note> are required to complete this operation, and one must take great care to avoid the smoke, as it is very harmful <note>Antimony vapors are toxic; early chemists were well aware of the respiratory dangers of these fumes.</note>.

When the powder has been dried in this manner, place it into a **Matrass** <note>A long-necked glass flask used for heating liquids.</note>, and pour **spirit of wine** <note>High-proof distilled alcohol.</note> over it until the liquid sits four fingers deep above the powder. Seal the vessel tightly and set it to digest in a **Bain-Marie** <note>A water bath used to provide gentle, uniform heat.</note> until the liquid turns red. Pour off the tinted spirit by inclination <note>Gently tilting the vessel to pour off the liquid without disturbing the sediment at the bottom.</note>, add fresh spirit, and digest as before; continue this process until the spirit no longer...

It is beneficial to take it as a precaution twice a year, in the Spring and in the Autumn.

### CHAPTER IX.

### Of Iron, or Mars.

#### Of the Crocus of Mars.

There are an infinite number of ways to extract the salt, or saffron of Mars <note>In alchemy, the "Saffron of Mars" (Crocus Martis) refers to various red or yellow oxides of iron used in medicine.</note>, most of which are long and laborious. Here is a very easy one. Take very clean **steel filings**, and place them in a large **faience** <note>A type of glazed earthenware or pottery.</note> dish: if you wish to make it **astringent** <note>A substance that constricts body tissues, used to stop bleeding or reduce secretions.</note>, pour **distilled vinegar** over them; if it is to be **aperitif** <note>In early modern medicine, "aperitive" remedies were intended to "open" obstructions in the internal organs, such as the liver or spleen.</note>, you shall use only common water. Expose your vessel to the sun, and stir it five or six times a day. And when your liquid has become red, pour it off by **inclination** <note>A method of decanting or pouring off a liquid to separate it from its sediment.</note> into a vessel while it is still cloudy, and put more vinegar or water upon the filings, and return it to the sun as before, and continue until you have as much of the tincture as you desire. Take all your tinctures,

### CHAPTER XI.

*Of Lead, or Saturn.* <note>In alchemical tradition, each of the seven metals was associated with a celestial body; lead was linked to the planet Saturn because of its heavy, dull, and cold nature.</note>

*Of the Salt, or Sugar of Lead.* <note>Known today as lead(II) acetate. It was called "sugar" due to its sweet taste, though it is highly toxic.</note>

WITHOUT taking the trouble to **calcine** <note>To heat a substance to high temperatures in the air to cause oxidation or to make it brittle.</note> the Lead, which is a long and arduous operation, one can easily find it at the **Spice Merchants** <note>original: "Espiciers" — in the 17th century, these merchants sold not only spices but also chemicals, dyes, and medicinal ingredients.</note>, and it is called ***Minium*** <note>Red lead (lead tetroxide), a bright red-orange pigment and chemical compound.</note>. Take as much as you wish and place it in a **Matrass** <note>A round-bottomed glass flask with a long neck, used for heating and distilling.</note>. Pour over it distilled vinegar until it sits three or four fingers' breadth above the powder; place the vessel in **digestion** <note>The process of exposing a substance to gentle, consistent heat to extract its active components.</note> in a **Bath** <note>A water bath or "bain-marie," used to ensure even, moderate heating without burning the contents.</note> at a gentle heat, and leave it there until the vinegar has acquired a **sweetness** <note>As the lead reacts with the acetic acid in the vinegar, it forms lead acetate, which has a distinctively sweet flavor.</note>. Then pour it off by **decantation** <note>original: "par inclination" — the act of tilting the vessel to pour off the liquid while leaving the solid sediment undisturbed.</note>, add more vinegar, and let it digest; continue this until the vinegar no longer draws out any sweetness. Take all your sweetened vinegar and filter it. If you wish to make the **magistery** <note>A refined precipitate or "master" preparation of a substance, believed to contain its essential medicinal power.</note> of Saturn, you will reserve a portion of the said vinegar, and evaporate

# FIFTH PART.

## FOREWORD.

**I** do not claim to take credit for the remedies I bring to light as being of my own composition: I confess that the greater part are from the prescriptions of very distinguished Physicians of the Faculty of Paris <note>The Faculty of Medicine of Paris was the most prestigious and authoritative medical body in 17th-century France, though often criticized for its strict adherence to traditional Greek and Roman medical theories.</note>, who charitably prescribed them for poor patients, and which I have put into practice; these have succeeded most fortunately. Another part was given to me by my friends. I cannot deny also that there are some of my own composition, the experience of which is very certain. I can assure this, having tested them all. In the preceding Treatises I have taught the ma- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "manière" (manner or method), continuing onto the next page.</note>

<summary>In this foreword to Part Five, the author establishes the credibility of his remedies by attributing them to the Physicians of the Faculty of Paris and his own successful clinical trials.</summary>
<keywords>medical authority, Faculty of Paris, clinical experience, charity, pharmaceutical recipes</keywords>

## Water that induces suppressed urine, & expels sand from the kidneys. <note>In historical medicine, "sand" refers to what we now call kidney stones or urinary gravel.</note>

TAKE twelve Lemons, and twelve Pomegranates, and cut them into slices, and put them in a copper Still <term>Alembic: A specialized vessel used for distillation</term>, with the following herbs; namely, Parsley, Lemon Balm, Hyssop, Saxifrage <note>The name "Saxifrage" literally means "stone-breaker," referring to its use against kidney stones</note>, Burnet, Philanthropos, or Cleavers <note>The text uses the Greek term "Philanthropos" (meaning "friend of man") and the French "Gratteron" for the same plant, *Galium aparine*, known for its sticky seeds</note>, two pounds of each: Pour over them twelve pints of common water; distill through the Cooling-coil <term>Refrigeratory: The part of the distilling apparatus that cools the vapor back into liquid</term> with a flaming fire at the start, which you will reduce when the water begins to distill. When you have four pints of water, stop. ### Water against the plague.

[Large initial P]RENEZ <note>TAKE</note> one pound of Scabious juice <note>Scabious (*Scabiosa*) was traditionally used to treat "scabs" and skin eruptions, making it a common ingredient in plague remedies</note>, one ounce of Provins Roses <note>The Apothecary's Rose (*Rosa gallica 'Officinalis'*), famous for its medicinal use in the town of Provins</note>, two ounces each of dried and grated lemon and orange peels, one ounce of Venice Theriac <term>Venice Theriac: A legendary and complex "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients including herbs, spices, and opium</term>, two drachms <term>Drachme: A unit of weight equal to one-eighth of an ounce</term> each of Cinnamon and Cloves: infuse everything for twenty-four hours in a pint <term>Pinte: The Paris pint, roughly 930 milliliters</term> of walnut water <note>A distilled water made from green walnut husks, believed to have purifying properties</note>; then distill over a gentle fire. One can take between one and two ounces in the morning on an empty stomach during times of plague. Furthermore, it strengthens cold and weak stomachs <note>In the humoral theory of the time, "cold" stomachs suffered from poor digestion and required "hot" spices like cinnamon and cloves to restore balance</note>.

### Specific water against the plague.

TAKE walnut husks, when they peel easily from the nut; leaves and tops of Rue <note>Rue (*Ruta graveolens*) was a staple of anti-plague recipes, believed to ward off "miasmatic" or corrupted air</note>, in equal parts: crush them in a stone mortar, and set them to ferment for three days in the cellar; then distill in the Water Bath <term>Bain-marie: A vessel of hot water used to heat the distillation flask gently and evenly</term>. One should take two spoonfuls every morning during times of plague. It is a sovereign preservative <note>In early modern medicine, a "sovereign" remedy was one considered highly effective or of the highest authority</note>.

### Water that cures pale colors.

TAKE Sage, Hyssop, Rosemary, and Savin <note>Savin (*Juniperus sabina*) was traditionally used to stimulate circulation and treat "women's obstructions," though it is toxic in large quantities</note>, one pound of each; crush them in a stone mortar, and set them to ferment for eight days in the cellar in a glass Cucurbit <term>Cucurbit: The gourd-shaped lower vessel of a distillation apparatus</term>, with four pounds of white wine; then you shall distill in a boiling Water Bath until the marc <term>Marc: The solid residue or pulp remaining after the liquid has been extracted</term> is dry. Take one pound of this water, and place within it half an ounce of Saffron of Mars <note>original: "Crocus Martis"</note> <term>Crocus Martis: An alchemical term for iron oxide (rust). In this context, it was used to treat "pale colors" (anemia) by introducing iron into the diet</term> in a small linen pouch. One shall take one ounce of this water every morning, up to two ounces. This must be continued for forty days; the Saffron of Mars will serve throughout <note>This indicates that the iron oxide in the pouch does not need to be replaced, as it slowly infuses the liquid over the forty-day course</note>.

<summary>This page provides two medicinal recipes: a preventative distilled water for the plague made from walnuts and rue, and a forty-day iron-infused treatment for "pale colors" or anemia.</summary>

### Water that brings about the purgations.
<note>"Purgations" in this context refers to the menstrual cycle, which humoral medicine viewed as a vital monthly cleansing of the body.</note>

**TAKE** Mugwort <note>original: "Armoiſe" (*Artemisia vulgaris*), a herb traditionally used to stimulate the uterus</note> and Hyssop, two handfuls of each; Saffron, Cinnamon, and Cloves, one drachm <term>Drachm: An apothecary weight approximately equal to 1/8th of an ounce or 3.9 grams</term> of each: put it all into a Cucurbit <term>Cucurbit: A gourd-shaped vessel used in distillation, referred to here as a "Courge"</term>, with four pounds of Spanish wine <note>Spanish wine, such as sack or sherry, was often used as a solvent in chemistry because of its high alcohol content</note>: in its absence, you shall take excellent white wine. One must drink this water for three consecutive days, evening and morning, taking two ounces each time.

## CHAPTER II.

### On Syrups.

### Syrup of Mercury-herb. <note>original: "Syrop de Mercuriale". This refers to the plant *Mercurialis annua* (annual mercury), used historically as a laxative and to "cleanse" the blood, rather than the chemical element mercury.</note>

**ONE MUST** take eight pounds of juice of Mercury-herb, and juices of Bugloss and Borage, two pounds of each: put them into a red copper basin with twelve pounds of Narbonne honey <note>Narbonne honey was highly prized in the 17th and 18th centuries for its quality and distinctive rosemary scent.</note>: boil for half an hour over a gentle fire, and skim the honey; then filter it through a Hippocras bag <term>Hippocras bag: A conical, sleeve-shaped filter made of flannel or linen, used to clarify liquids.</term>. Take four ounces of Gentian roots and half a pound of Flame-Iris roots <note>original: "racines de Flambe". These are the roots of the Iris plant, often used for respiratory or digestive ailments.</note>; cut them into slices and set them to infuse for twenty-four hours over hot ashes in three pounds of white wine; filter the said infusion without pressing it <note>Filtering "without expression" (pressing) ensured a clearer liquid, as squeezing the dregs could release unwanted bitterness or sediment.</note>, and combine it with the ingredients mentioned above; then cook them to the consistency of a syrup, which can be kept for one year, for it is necessary to take a spoonful of it every morning throughout the year at...
