Charitable & Easy [Chemistry]. 203

...on an empty stomach. The entire composition described above is for one person only. This syrup strengthens, purges, purifies the blood, and cools <note>original: "rafraichit". In humoral medicine, a "cooling" remedy was intended to counteract "heat" or inflammation in the body.</note>: it preserves youth: there are almost no illnesses against which it does not serve as a remedy. It is necessary to be purged before using it. It may be harmful to those with spleen ailments <term>ratteleux: individuals suffering from disorders of the spleen (la rate), often associated in this period with melancholy or congestion.</term>, because of the large quantity of honey of which it is composed.

### Syrup to induce sleep.

**TAKE** juice of purslane <note>original: "pourpier". A succulent plant traditionally used for its cooling and soothing properties.</note> and of lettuce, two pounds of each; water-lily flowers and field poppy flowers—otherwise called *Rhoeas* or corn-poppies <note>original: "Rheas, ou Coquelicos". These are the common red poppies of the fields, used since antiquity as a mild sedative.</note>—half a pound of each: boil the juices and the flowers together for about a quarter of an hour; then filter through a straining bag <term>chausse: A conical cloth filter, also known as a Hippocras bag, used for clarifying medicinal syrups.</term>, and add sugar, pound for pound, and cook to the consistency of a syrup. One must skim all syrups toward the end of their cooking. One shall take an ounce of it in the evening when going to bed, in a glass of barley water: it cools, induces sleep, and is excellent against inflammations of the lungs.

ONE must take sage juice, and betony <note>original: "Betoine". ### Ptisan that purifies the blood.

YOU will take roots and leaves of Chicory, Blessed Thistle <note>original: "Chardon-benit." Traditional medical practice emphasized that cleansing or "reducing" treatments should coincide with the moon's decreasing phase.</note>.

A potent and dangerous plant used in antiquity and the early modern period to treat "madness" and brain-related ailments.</note>,

The dose is one drachm.

The dose is one scruple <term>scruple: an apothecary weight, roughly 1.3 grams.</term>.

### *Pills that purge the waters of the dropsical.* <note>original: "hydropiques." In early medicine, dropsy (now called edema) was seen as an accumulation of watery humors in the body's cavities or tissues, often treated with powerful diuretics and purgatives.</note>

*Balm or Ointment against burns, & against the marks of smallpox.*

Early modern medicine sought fatty, "filling" ointments to smooth the skin's appearance.</note>, one must apply it four or five times a day, like an ointment, and continue for one month.

## Balm against the pains of cold gout. <note>In the humoral theory of medicine, "cold" gout was characterized by a lack of heat and circulation in the joints, often worsening in damp or wintry conditions. The ingredients here (hyssop, juniper, and parsley) were considered "warming" agents to counteract this condition.</note>

TAKE roots and leaves of **Hyssop**, and of **Parsley**, two pounds of each; remove the fibers <note>original: "cordes". You shall boil the whole until the wine is consumed <note>This indicates the mixture should be boiled until the water and alcohol in the wine have evaporated, leaving the herbal essence infused within the fat.</note>, then press it out. This balm is applied in the same manner as described above. It is good against all pains that proceed from the cold.

## Balm that stops diarrhea and the flux of blood. <note>The "flux of blood" usually refers to dysentery, a severe form of diarrhea accompanied by blood, which was a common and dangerous ailment in the early modern period.</note>

YOU shall take a half-pound of dried **Provins roses** <note>Provins roses (*Rosa gallica officinalis*) were highly prized in pharmacy for their astringent properties, which were believed to "bind" or tighten the bowels.</note>, one ounce of **Venice treacle** <term>Venice treacle: Also known as *theriaca*, this was a legendary, complex medicine containing dozens of ingredients (including opium and viper flesh). It was used as a universal antidote and a potent treatment for internal distress.</term>, and two pounds of full-bodied red wine: boil the whole until reduced by half, then press it out. A cloth must be soaked in the said balm and applied very hot to the belly, and renewed two or three times a day. It also stops vomiting when applied over the stomach.

### Sovereign balm to heal all kinds of wounds.

TAKE juice of **Betony** and **St. John’s Wort**, half a pound of each; **Oil of Petroleum** <term>Oil of Petroleum: A naturally occurring mineral oil or naphtha, often used in early medicine for its supposed ability to "dry out" and disinfect wounds.</term> one ounce, **washed turpentine** two ounces, **olive oil** six ounces, **Mastic** and **Honey**, one dram <term>Dram: A unit of weight in the apothecaries' system, equivalent to about 1/8th of an ounce or roughly 3.9 grams.</term> of each: boil everything over a low fire in a glazed pot until the juices have been consumed <note>original: "juſques à la conſomption des ſucs". This refers to boiling the mixture until the water content of the fresh plant juices has evaporated, leaving only the medicinal essence in the oil.</note>; then squeeze it out, and place the liquid in a vial for fifteen days in the sun. To apply this balm, it must be slightly warmed and placed into the wound with a small cloth on top, soaked in the said balm; in twenty-four hours it causes wounds to begin healing. It also removes black marks and bruises.

For this purpose, after having pierced them, one shall use the soap plaster, which will draw and dry the area.

250                *Chemistry*

Plaster for cuts, chapping, and fissures.

TAKE two pounds of rose oil, a pound and a half of ceruse <note>original: "Ceruse"; white lead, a substance commonly used in historical medicine for its drying and cooling properties, despite its high toxicity.</note> in an extremely fine powder, and four ounces of white wax: incorporate everything together in a tin vessel over a low flame, until it reaches the consistency of a plaster. It must be stirred constantly until it is finished: it heals abrasions and cracks that occur on the breasts, hands, and heels: it heals all kinds of cuts and abrasions.

# SIXTH PART.

## FOREWORD.

I have added this Part to my Book for the benefit of Ladies, to protect them from an infinite number of accidents <note>In this context, "accidents" refers to adverse reactions, skin irritations, or unintended side effects caused by improperly prepared cosmetics.</note> that occur when they apply things to their faces without knowing their composition. I am simplifying the procedures and explaining myself as clearly as possible, to teach them how to make the things they need themselves: They shall choose the Waters <note>term: "Eaux" (Waters); these were typically distilled floral or herbal essences used as skin tonics or cleansers.</note> and the Pomades <note>term: "Pommades" (Ointments); scented creams or salves often used for moisturizing or masking blemishes.</note> that are suitable for them; for what is good for one complexion is not for another. One must nourish and moisturize delicate and thin complexions: this is why they require Waters of

<summary>This page introduces the sixth part of the manual, dedicated to cosmetic chemistry for women, emphasizing the importance of self-made products to avoid harmful commercial ingredients.</summary>
<keywords>cosmetics, skin care, complexion, foreword, waters, pomades, women's health, early modern chemistry</keywords>

flesh, and of milk, or pomades. <note>The previous page's list of ingredients for delicate complexions continues here, likely referring to distilled waters made from veal or other "flesh" and milky emulsions intended to soothe the skin.</note>

For fat persons, who have an oily complexion, they must be dried out; for this effect, waters where some acids are included, such as distilled vinegar, lemon juice, or Queen of Hungary water <note>term: "Eau de la Reine de Hongrie" (Queen of Hungary Water); a famous distilled tonic made from rosemary and spirits, used both as a perfume and a medicinal skin treatment.</note>, are good for them. And even for coarse complexions, which must be cleansed <note>original: "deterger"</note> and corroded <note>In the context of early modern cosmetics, "corroding" referred to a mild chemical exfoliation intended to thin or refine a thickened, "coarse" skin texture.</note> to make the skin more delicate, they shall apply these often on their faces in the beginning to set their complexion; then they shall maintain and nourish it with some water or pomade, as they shall judge appropriate.

Above all, I advise Ladies to put as little Camphor as possible into compositions for the face; for it spoils and causes the loss of teeth, and causes many fluxions <note>term: "fluxions"; a historical medical term for the abnormal flow or accumulation of bodily fluids (humors), often manifesting as inflammation or discharge in the gums, eyes, or respiratory tract.</note>. As for Mercury, Sublimate <note>term: "Sublimé" (Corrosive Sublimate); mercuric chloride, a highly toxic substance used as a skin whitener that caused severe long-term poisoning.</note>, and Bismuth <note>original: "Eſtain de glace"; literally "ice-tin," a period term for Bismuth, which was commonly used in white face powders and paints.</note>, I advise not to use them in any way; besides the fact that they erase the beauty of the face through long use, they produce very grievous, and sometimes incurable, diseases: This is what Ladies must take care— <note>The text cuts off mid-word "garde" (care/heed), continuing on the next page.</note>

->CHAPTER I.<-

->Of simple distilled waters for the beau-<-
->tification of the face.<-

The simples <term>simples; medicinal plants used on their own rather than as part of a complex mixture</term> from which one draws waters for the face are Plantain, Silverweed <note>original: "Argentine"; *Potentilla anserina*, named for the silvery underside of its leaves and traditionally used as a skin tonic.</note>, Water Lily, Stinging Nettle, and Bryony <note>original: "Couleuvrée"; referring to *Bryonia alba*, sometimes called "snake-root" or "wild hops."</note>; the leaves of which must be crushed, fermented, pressed, and distilled in a Bain-Marie <term>Bain-Marie; a water bath used to provide gentle, even heat for distillation, preventing the plant matter from burning.</term>. These waters must be exposed to the sun to preserve them.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For flowers, they must be put into a cucurbit <term>cucurbit; a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation that holds the liquid to be heated.</term> while freshly picked, then distilled in a Bain-Marie, without adding any solvent <note>original: "menstruë"; a term used by early chemists for a solvent or liquid medium used to extract the virtues of a plant.</note> to them: it is enough to press them a little. Usually, one takes the flowers of Rosemary, Almond, Peach, Broad Bean <note>original: "Febves"; bean flower water was a highly prized cosmetic in the 17th century for whitening the skin and removing freckles.</note>, Elderberry, Lily of the Valley, Linden, Lily, and Marsh-mallows.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The waters that are distilled from fruits for the face are Green Walnuts, Strawberries, Lemons, Melons, and Cucumbers.

### CHAPTER III.

### Of Compound Waters to preserve & embellish the face.

### Water for coarse complexions. ###

TAKE a dozen lemons that have thin rinds, the whites of a dozen fresh hard-boiled eggs, from which the yolks have been removed: cut the lemons and egg whites into rounds, and place them in a glass <term>cucurbit</term> <note>The lower, gourd-shaped vessel of a distillation apparatus used to hold the liquid being heated.</note>, at the bottom of which you have placed one pound of well-washed Venice Turpentine <note>A resin obtained from the larch tree, prized in early medicine and cosmetics for its cleansing and healing properties.</note>; then distill in a boiling Water Bath <note>A double boiler or bain-marie.</note>, and place the resulting water in the sun. This water cleanses and softens the skin, and whitens it. It should be used for washing every evening and morning.

### Another water for coarse complexions. ###

YOU must take one pound of almond blossoms dried in the shade, half a pound of squash blossoms, one pound of lily blossoms, six lemons cut into slices, the whites and shells of two dozen fresh eggs, and one pint <note>original: "chopine." A French unit of volume roughly equivalent to 475ml.</note> of white wine: let the whole mixture infuse for one night, then distill in a boiling Water Bath until the <term>residue</term> <note>original: "feces." In early chemistry, this refers to the solid dregs or sediment remaining in the vessel after distillation.</note> remains dry. This water evens out the complexion.

### *Water against scars and marks of the smallpox.* ###

TAKE roots of <term>Wild Cucumbers</term> <note>original: "Concombres sauvages." Likely *Ecballium elaterium*, known for its potent purging properties but used here topically.</note>, and of <term>Iris</term> <note>original: "Flambe." The Iris, particularly its root, was frequently used in early modern cosmetics for its pleasant scent and skin-clearing properties.</note>, half a pound of each; roots of <term>Marshmallows</term> <note>original: "Guimauves." Valued for their soothing mucilage which softens the skin.</note>, and <term>White Lily bulbs</term> <note>original: "oignons de Lys blancs." The Madonna lily was a classic remedy for treating skin blemishes and softening scars.</note>, one pound of each; half a pound of ripe Grapes, <term>Broad bean leaves</term> <note>original: "fueilles de Febves."</note>, and <term>Pellitory-of-the-wall</term> <note>original: "Parietaire." A common plant in historical medicine used for its cooling and anti-inflammatory effects.</note>, one handful of each; <term>Water-lily</term> <note>original: "Nenuphar."</note> and Mallow flowers, two handfuls of each; one pound of barley bread crumb: infuse the whole in a <term>pint</term> <note>The French "pinte" varied by region but was generally around 950ml; a significant volume for this recipe.</note> of white wine, and a pint of goat's milk: you shall add to the infusion a radish cut into slices, and half an ounce each of the <term>four cold seeds</term> <note>original: "quatre semences froides." A standard pharmaceutical set typically comprising the seeds of cucumber, melon, gourd, and watermelon, prized for their cooling and moisturizing properties.</note>; half a pound of urine from a young girl of nine or ten years old <note>Urine was a common ingredient in early modern chemistry and cosmetics, valued for its ammonia content which acted as a cleansing agent and solvent.</note>: distill the whole in a boiling <term>Water Bath</term> <note>original: "Bain boüillant." A distillation method using indirect heat to preserve the delicate properties of the botanical ingredients.</note>. This water is excellent against all sorts of spots that appear on the face; it removes scars, and erases the marks of <term>smallpox</term> <note>original: "petite verolle." Smallpox often left survivors with permanent pitted scars or "pockmarks."</note> and of burns.

*Water that whitens the face.*

YOU shall take the shells of twelve fresh eggs, one pound of <term>sap of the vine</term> <note>original: "eau de pleurs de vigne." Literally "vine tears," this is the sap that leaks from a vine when pruned in the spring, traditionally used as a skin tonic.</note>, one ounce of common salt, and half a pound of spring water: distill the whole by the <term>Retort</term> <note>original: "Cornuë." A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids.</note> in a <term>sand bath</term> <note>original: "feu de sable." A method where the distillation vessel is placed in a container of sand which is then heated, providing a steady, even temperature.</note>. This water <term>corrodes</term> <note>In this early modern context, "corrode" refers to a chemical peel or exfoliating effect intended to strip away the outer layer of skin to reveal a lighter complexion.</note> the skin, and renders the complexion white and delicate. It must be applied for eight days, morning and evening; thereafter one shall apply it only once a week.

*Water against freckles and redness of the face.*

TAKE white vinegar, rose-water, and lemon juice, one <term>pound</term> <note>The OCR reads "li-mon," but based on the context of measurements in this volume, it is almost certainly "livre" (pound).</note> of each; four ounces of <term>native sulfur</term> <note>original: "Soufre vif." A furnace arrangement where the fire is built in a circle around the vessel to provide intense, radiating heat.</note>. One must often wash the freckles and redness with this water, and place a <insert>linen cloth</insert> <note>The OCR reads "liuge," a typo for "linge" (linen).</note> soaked therein upon the face during the night.

# CHAPTER IV.

## On the oil, & water of Talc.

### Oil of Talc.

MANY assert that they know
how to extract the oil of Talc without
any addition; this operation is not so
common, nor so easy as they claim:
I believe that few persons
know this secret, and that it is very rare.
<note>The "Oil of Talc" was a legendary cosmetic in the early modern period, often sought by alchemists. It was believed to be a "fixed" substance that could permanently whiten and preserve the skin.</note>
Here are some methods of preparing
the Talc, which are good for the complexion,
and are easy to make, and I can assure
you that their effect is very good.
   Take three or four dozen
fresh eggs, which you shall hard-boil, and
remove the shells, and cut them in
half, remove the yolks, and put
in their place some powdered <term>Venetian Talc</term> <note>original: "Talc de Venise." A high-quality, translucent form of mica or foliated talc, prized for its pearlescent luster and used as a base for the finest face powders.</note> pulve-

rized, and sifted; then you shall rejoin the two halves together, and bind them with white thread, and put a little <term>sealing wax</term> <note>original: "cire d'Espagne." Literally "Spanish wax," a resin-based wax used to hermetically seal joints in glassware.</note> over the thread, across from the joints to make them hold: you shall arrange all the eggs, thus prepared, in a glass <term>cucurbite</term> <note>A gourd-shaped chemical vessel used for heating and distillation.</note>, and cover it with another small cucurbite: you shall <term>lute</term> <note>original: "lutterez." To seal the joint between two vessels with a paste (here made of lime and egg white) to prevent the escape of vapors.</note> them together with quicklime and egg white; then place them in horse manure, so that they are surrounded on all sides by the thickness of one foot of the said manure. <note>Fermenting horse manure was a standard laboratory tool in the early modern period, used as a "natural furnace" to provide a steady, gentle, and moist heat for long-term digestion.</note> When the manure begins to cool, it will be necessary to renew it: and to stimulate it, it must occasionally be sprinkled with a little warm water. ### Another oil of Talc.

**YOU** shall take four ounces of pulverized Venice Talc <note>Venice Talc was the highest grade of talc available to early modern chemists, favored for its purity and used extensively in "waters" and oils to whiten the skin.</note>, one pound of wheat flour paste, from which you shall make thirty-two pieces, and put in the middle of each piece one <term>drachm</term> <note>original: "un gros." You shall put all the small loaves in the oven to bake with the large bread loaves <note>Baking the talc inside dough was a method of "gentle calcination," protecting the mineral from direct fire while using the heat of the oven to alter its properties.</note>. When they are baked, you shall open them and take the Talc that will be in the middle, which you shall pulverize; then you shall take <term>bryony</term> roots <note>original: "racines de Coulevrées." White bryony (*Bryonia dioica*), often called "snake-root" in French, was used in alchemy and medicine for its ability to "draw" or "attract" moisture.</note>, in which you shall make hollows the depth of four fingers, which you shall fill by a third with the Talc that you have pulverized. Afterward, you shall place all your roots thus filled in the cellar, and leave them there for twenty-four hours; at the end of which you will find them filled with a liquor that

->### Another oil of Talc.<-

**TAKE** four ounces of pulverized Talc, and put them with an equal part of saltpeter <note>original: "salpetre." Potassium nitrate, a powerful salt used in alchemy to help break down and "open" minerals through heat.</note> into a Crucible in the middle of glowing coals. When the saltpeter is consumed, you shall add more, and repeat this five or six times; then let it cool. Take the mass that remains in the Crucible and reduce it to powder, and put it into a small cloth bag, which you shall suspend in the cellar; place a vessel beneath to receive the liquor which will fall by <term>deliquescence</term> <note>original: "par defaillance." Literally "by failing" or "fainting." In early chemistry, this refers to a solid substance absorbing enough moisture from the damp cellar air to melt into a liquid form.</note>. It must be applied once every week when going to bed. It whitens and cleanses the <term>skin</term> <note>original: "le cuir." While modern French uses "cuir" for leather, in this period it was commonly used to refer to the human skin or complexion.</note> of all kinds of spots.

Talc Water.

**TAKE** in the month of May such a quantity of snails with their shells as you please, and put them into an earthen pot with a handful of salt, and as much vinegar as is needed to cover the snails by a finger's width: stir everything together to make them discharge <note>original: "dégorger." This refers to the process of making the snails expel slime and impurities.</note> and cast off their froth: then remove them, and wash them three or four times in succession in white wine; then wipe them with a white cloth, and put them into a glazed earthen pot. Give them every day, for three months, a spoonful of sifted <term>Talc</term> <note>original: "Talc." A soft mineral prized in early modern cosmetics for its ability to provide a pearly, white luster to the skin.</note> powder, and stir them sometimes, and push down those that would climb up, and cover the pot. If a small film forms at the opening of their shells, it does not matter. After three months, take the snails and everything that shall be in the pot; crush everything, including the shells, and put them into a glass <term>cucurbit</term> <note>original: "Cucurbite." A rounded, gourd-shaped chemical vessel used as the base of a distillation apparatus.</note>, and distill in a boiling <term>bath</term> <note>original: "Bain boüillant." This refers to a *Bain-Marie* or water bath, used to apply a controlled heat that does not exceed the boiling point of water.</note> until all the <term>feces</term> <note>original: "feces." In early chemistry, this term refers to the "dregs" or the solid, spent material left over after a liquid has been extracted or distilled.</note> are completely dry: remove the

*Ointment to keep the complexion fresh*
*& smooth.*

YOU will take May butter <note>original: "beure de May." Butter produced in May was highly valued in early modern pharmacy and cosmetics, as it was believed to contain the medicinal virtues of spring herbs eaten by the cattle.</note>, and bull tallow, half a pound of each: melt them together in a new pot, with half a <term>septier</term> <note>original: "septier." An old French liquid measure; here it likely refers to a half-pint or approximately 240ml.</note> of Rose-water, then strain them through a cloth into another pot in which there is half a septier of Rose-water. When the whole mixture is cold, you will lift it out with a spoon, and place it into a marble mortar, and incorporate into it six ounces of <term>ceruse</term> <note>original: "Ceruse." Also known as white lead (lead carbonate). A popular but highly toxic pigment used in the 17th century to whiten the skin.</note> that has been well washed several times in Rose-water, dried in the sun, and reduced to an impalpable powder; then store it in a very clean pot, and place sugar over it, as was previously described. You will apply this ointment morning and evening, and you will wipe it off an hour after having applied it.

**Pomade to nourish the complexion, &**
**against the marks of**
**smallpox.** <note>original: "petite verolle." Smallpox was a devastating and common disease in the 17th century; those who survived were often left with deep facial pitting or "pockmarks," making cosmetic treatments for these scars highly sought after.</note>

Short for *bain-marie*, a method of indirect heating that prevents the delicate ingredients from burning.</note>

*Another excellent pomade.*

TAKE one ounce of fine, very white pearls, and crush them in a mortar, and place them in a glass vessel; pour over them distilled white vinegar so that it floats above them by two finger-breadths: place the vessel on hot ashes <note>A "sand bath" or bed of ashes was used to provide gentle, indirect heat, preventing the glass from shattering or the ingredients from boiling too fiercely.</note> to assist in the dissolution of the said pearls. When they have dissolved, you will pour off the vinegar by tilting the vessel, and wash the dissolved substance <term>dissolution: here refers to the pearl matter that has been softened or liquefied by the acid</term> two or three times with orange flower water or rose water; then you will incorporate it with two parts of the sheep’s foot pomade mentioned above. In the absence of the dissolved pearls, one may mix with the said pomade some Venetian Talc <note>original: "Talc de Venise." A high-quality form of mica or magnesium silicate used in historical cosmetics to give the skin a pearlescent, smooth glow.</note> reduced to an impalpable powder. This pomade is applied evening and morning, and is most excellent.

        *Another pomade.*

TAKE a dozen Api apples <note>original: "pommes d’Api." A very old variety of small, decorative dessert apple, often called "Lady Apples" in English. They were prized for their fragrance and bright red color.</note>, which you will peel, core, and cut into slices, and let them infuse for an entire day in Rose-water with an ounce of Cinnamon water, in such a way that the water covers the apples to the depth of a finger’s height: take six ounces of pork leaf fat <term>panne de porc: the high-quality fat surrounding the kidneys, used as a base for ointments because it can be purified to be odorless and white.</term>, remove the fibers and skins, as has been described, and cut them into pieces, and put everything into a glazed pot, and melt over a small fire, then boil over a low heat until the apples are well cooked; then pass through a strainer bag <note>original: "chauſſe." A conical fabric filter used by apothecaries to clarify liquids.</note>, with a bowl underneath containing a pint <note>original: "chopine." A pre-revolutionary French liquid measure roughly equal to 476 milliliters, or slightly less than a modern US pint.</note> of Rose-water, let it cool, then lift out the pomade with a spoon, and wash it, purify <note>original: "dulcorez." In early modern pharmacy, to dulcorate meant to wash a substance in fresh water to remove impurities or "acridity," rather than to add sugar.</note> it, and pound it until it is very white; incorporate a few grains of musk, ambergris, or civet <note>These three substances—derived from the musk deer, the sperm whale, and the civet cat respectively—were the primary "fixatives" of the 17th century, used to make the floral scents of the pomade last longer on the skin.</note>, and store it in a very clean and covered pot. This pomade is good for the face, for the hands, and the hair: It is applied in the evening.

**Ointment against angry tetters.**
<note>original: "dartres vives." In early modern medicine, "dartre" (often translated as "tetter") was a general term for various inflammatory skin conditions, including eczema, herpes, or shingles. "Vives" (live/active) implies the rash is red, inflamed, or weeping.</note>

**TAKE** one ounce of Spanish flies <term>Cantarides: dried and powdered blister beetles (*Lytta vesicatoria*). The rendered fat of sheep, commonly used as a stable base for medicinal salves.</note> well washed and purified in Rose-water: melt it, and incorporate the powder of Spanish flies into it, and remove it from the heat, and stir constantly until the ointment is cold. An ointment made from the fat of a young goat, prized for being gentler and more soothing than mutton or beef fat.</note>.

*Ointment against wrinkles of the*

YOU shall take juice of white Lily bulbs <note>White lily (*Lilium candidum*) was a staple of early modern beauty recipes, believed to whiten the skin and clear blemishes.</note>, and Narbonne honey <note>original: "miel de Narbonne." *Another ointment against wrinkles*
*of the face.*

TAKE six fresh eggs, and boil them until they are hard; remove the yolks, and in their place put Myrrh <note>Myrrh is a resinous sap from trees of the *Commiphora* genus, used since antiquity for its fragrance and perceived healing properties for the skin.</note>, and powdered sugar candy, in equal parts: rejoin the eggs, and place them on a plate before the fire: a liquid will come out of them which you shall incorporate with one ounce of pork fat <note>original: "graisse de porc." Lard was frequently used as a base for ointments because of its consistency and ability to be absorbed by the skin.</note>. It must be applied in the morning, and left to dry, and then wiped off.

## Another ointment against wrinkles.

TAKE oil of wax <note>Oil of wax was produced by the dry distillation of beeswax; it was valued for its emollient properties.</note>, spirit of Turpentine, spermaceti <note>original: "semence de baleine" (whale seed). This refers to spermaceti, a waxy substance found in the head of sperm whales, used to give ointments a smooth, pearly consistency.</note>, one ounce of each: melt one pound of deer tallow <note>Stag or deer tallow was considered superior to beef or mutton tallow in historical pharmacy for its supposed ability to penetrate and nourish the skin.</note> in a new glazed pot with four ounces of rose water; then incorporate the aforementioned drugs, and pass them through a cloth into an earthenware bowl containing rose water, or some other good water; let it cool, then remove it, and pound the ointment as previously described. ## Ointment against freckles and spots.

TAKE ox gall <note>original: "fiel de beuf." Animal bile was frequently used in early modern recipes as a powerful detergent or solvent to "cleanse" the skin of blemishes.</note>, spirit of Sulfur <note>original: "esprit de Soufre." Historically, this referred to sulfuric acid. In a cosmetic context, it would have been highly diluted to act as a chemical peel.</note>, burnt sponge <note>Burnt sponge was a staple of the historical pharmacopeia, later discovered to be effective due to its high iodine content.</note>, mutton tallow, in equal parts, one dram of each: incorporate the whole together, and make an ointment of it. It must be applied in the evening upon going to bed on the freckles and spots <note>original: "lentilles & rousseurs." These terms refer respectively to lentigo (small dark spots) and general reddish skin blotches or freckles.</note>, and wash in the morning with strawberry water <note>Distilled strawberry water was a popular cosmetic wash believed to brighten the complexion and clear the skin.</note>.

## Another ointment against freckles
## & spots.

YOU shall take two Capendu apples <note>original: "pommes de Capendu." The Court-pendu apple is one of the oldest known French varieties, valued in the 17th century for its fragrance and firm flesh.</note>, Celery, and Fennel, one handful of each; two drams of barley flour: boil the whole together for a quarter of an hour in four ounces of Rosewater; then add one ounce of fine barley flour, the whites of four fresh eggs, and one ounce of deer fat <note>original: "graisse de Cerf." Deer or stag fat was a common ingredient in early modern cosmetics, believed to have superior emollient and skin-clearing properties compared to beef or mutton fat.</note>; pass the whole through a straining cloth <note>original: "étamine." A thin, loosely woven fabric used for filtering liquids in pharmacy and cooking.</note> into a bowl in which there is a little Rosewater; wash, sweeten <note>original: "dulcorez." In historical chemistry and pharmacy, to 'dulcify' or 'sweeten' meant to wash a substance repeatedly in water to remove salts, acidity, or impurities.</note>, and pound as you did for the other ointments. Afterward, one must guard oneself against the sun, the open air, and tanning <note>original: "hâle." This refers to the darkening or coarsening of the skin caused by exposure to the elements.</note>.

*Ointment against black, white, red, & greenish spots*
*that appear on the face.*

TAKE lemon juice and egg whites, in equal parts; beat them very strongly together, put them in a ceramic pot <note>original: "terrine." A shallow earthenware or glazed clay vessel used for mixing or cooking pharmaceutical preparations.</note> over the fire, with a little chicken fat, and stir constantly until the whole reaches the consistency of an ointment. It must be applied every evening, and continued until the said spots have disappeared.

### Another red for the face.

**YOU** shall take one ounce of powdered Brazilwood <note>original: "bois de Bresil." A red wood from tropical trees, widely used in the early modern period to create dyes and inks.</note>, and set it to infuse for twenty-four hours over hot ashes in strong distilled vinegar, in such a way that the vinegar surpasses the said powder by two fingers <note>A traditional measurement referring to the width of a person's fingers.</note>. After this, you shall add two pounds <note>original: "livres." A pre-revolutionary French unit of weight, approximately 489 grams.</note> of water, and boil the whole mixture until it is reduced by three quarters <note>original: "diminution des trois parties." Meaning three-fourths of the liquid has evaporated.</note>. When this is done, you shall add half a quarteron <note>A "quarteron" is a quarter of a pound; half a quarteron is roughly 61 grams.</note> of powdered alum <note>A mineral salt used as a mordant to fix the dye to a surface.</note>, and half an ounce of isinglass <note>original: "colle de poisson." A gelatinous substance obtained from fish bladders, used here as a binder to give the makeup consistency.</note> cut into pieces. When it has dissolved, strain the mixture and place it into boxes. It is applied as described above.

### Another red.

**TAKE** one ounce of well-pounded red sandalwood <note>original: "Sandal rouge." A fragrant wood used for its deep red color.</note>, and pour over it one pound of distilled vinegar. Boil it until it is reduced by three quarters, then add a pinch of powdered alum, and two spoonfuls of rose water in which gum tragacanth <note>original: "gomme Adragant." A natural sap used as a thickener and stabilizer in cosmetics.</note> has been dissolved. Strain it and put it into a box.

# CHAPTER VIII.

## Ox Gall.

### How it is prepared in Montpellier.

TAKE four or five ox galls, and empty them into a large earthenware bowl; add alum and glass salt <term>glass salt: "sel de verre," also known as sandiver, is a byproduct of glassmaking. It was often used in early modern chemistry and medicine as a scouring or purifying agent.</term> reduced to powder, one ounce of each: whip the whole mixture with a bundle of twigs <note>original: "une poignée de verge," referring to a small whisk made of thin rods or switches.</note> for the space of two or three hours, until the whole becomes like foam; then you will filter it through a piece of cloth, and let it pass through at its leisure <note>Meaning to let it drip through slowly without forcing it.</note>: you will take what has passed through, and put it in a double-glass vial <note>A vial made of thicker or reinforced glass to prevent breakage during the chemical reactions or temperature changes.</note>, with two ounces of Borax, two drams <term>dram: "gros," a historical French unit of weight equal to 1/8th of an ounce, or roughly 3.8 grams.</term> of Camphor, and one dram of Sublimate <note>original: "Sublimé." This refers to mercuric chloride, a highly toxic substance that was historically used in very small quantities in cosmetics for its potent—though dangerous—skin-whitening properties.</note>; expose the vial to the sun for fifteen days, and shake it three or four times a day; then you will place the said vial in a pit in the cellar, in such a way that it is completely covered with earth, and leave it there for forty days; then

<summary>This page begins a recipe for a preparation of ox gall according to the traditions of Montpellier, a major center for medicine and perfumery. The process involves a lengthy period of whisking followed by "aging" the mixture by burying it in a cellar for forty days.</summary>
<keywords>ox gall, Montpellier, cosmetics, chemistry, borax, camphor, corrosive sublimate, sandiver, glass salt</keywords>

## Yellow day-cornettes.
TAKE an egg yolk and four spoonfuls of spirit of wine <note>Spirit of wine (*esprit de vin*) is high-proof distilled alcohol, commonly used in early modern chemistry as a solvent and to facilitate quick drying.</note>; beat them well together with a spoon, and soak your cornette <note>A cornette was a fashionable 17th-century women’s indoor cap or headdress, often featuring lace or linen lappets that hung down the sides of the face.</note> in it, which you will have first cleansed three or four times with rosewater; and then let them dry in the shade.

## Another way to yellow cornettes.
TAKE the inner bark <note>original: "seconde écorce." In early botany, the "second bark" refers to the cambium or inner layer of the bark, which contains the most potent dyes or medicinal properties.</note> of barberry wood, or *Berberis*; soak it in river water for twenty-four hours: when you see the water has turned yellow, soak your cornettes in it, and set them to dry without squeezing them.

## Another.
YOU will take ten grains of saffron, and pour an ounce of spirit of wine over it; and when the spirit of wine is well colored, you will pour it off by decanting <note>original: "par inclination." This is the laboratory practice of tilting a vessel to pour off the liquid while leaving the solid sediment undisturbed at the bottom.</note>, and add more [spirit], and continue until your saffron yields no more dye: mix <note>The text ends here as a catchword, suggesting the instructions for mixing the dyes continue on the following page.</note>

### CHAPTER XI.
## Of pastes, waters, and pomades for the hands.

### *Paste for the hands.*

TAKE four ounces each of the four cold seeds <note>The "four greater cold seeds" were a standard pharmaceutical grouping in the 17th century, consisting of the seeds of cucumber, melon, gourd, and pumpkin, valued for their cooling and moisturizing properties.</note>, hulled; two ounces of pine nuts; and half a pound of peeled sweet almonds. First, crush the four cold seeds; when they have become a paste, add the pine nuts and incorporate them; then, add the almonds little by little. It requires at least four hours of pounding to properly crush the whole mixture. Then, add two fresh egg yolks; incorporate everything well together, and the paste will be finished. Put it into a jar with a little sugar on top; it keeps for a long time. One must rub the hands with it every morning, then wipe them with a white cloth.

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Chapter XI, transitioning from textile care to personal cosmetics. It provides a labor-intensive recipe for a moisturizing hand paste made from crushed seeds, nuts, and egg yolks.</summary>
<keywords>hand paste, cold seeds, pine nuts, sweet almonds, egg yolks, 17th-century cosmetics, skincare, manual labor in pharmacy</keywords>

### *Another paste for the hands.*

TAKE one pound of peeled sweet almonds, one ounce of Orris powder <note>Orris powder, made from the root of the Iris flower, was prized in the 17th century for its violet-like scent and its ability to act as a thickening agent in cosmetics.</note>, four ounces of pine nuts, and one ounce of spermaceti <note>original: "semence de Baleine." While literally "whale seed," this refers to spermaceti, a waxy substance obtained from sperm whales, used extensively in historical ointments for its emollient properties.</note>: crush everything well together until it reaches the consistency of a paste. Incorporate two ounces of oil of the four cold seeds <note>The "four cold seeds" (cucumber, melon, gourd, and pumpkin) were discussed on the previous page as a cooling base for skin treatments.</note> and the yolks of two fresh eggs. Boil the mixture in a skillet with a half-septier <note>A "septier" was a traditional French liquid measure; a half-septier is approximately 240 milliliters or about half a pint.</note> of rosewater, stirring constantly with a spatula until the paste no longer sticks to the skillet.
One must rub the hands with it every morning and evening.

### Another pomade for the hands.

**I**t is necessary to take the whites of six eggs, and purify them as described above <note>This refers to the process on page 316 involving straining the whites through a sponge to remove the foam.</note>, then incorporate them with starch and powdered ceruse <term>Ceruse (white lead): a white pigment widely used in historical cosmetics to whiten the skin, though now known to be highly toxic due to its lead content.</term>, one ounce of each; two ounces of the oil of the four cold seeds <note>The "four greater cold seeds" in early modern pharmacy typically included cucumber, melon, gourd, and watermelon; their oils were valued for their "cooling" and moisturizing properties.</note>: put everything into a small earthen pot over the fire, and stir with a spatula until it reaches the consistency of a pomade. ### Water for whitening the hands.

**T**AKE one pound of henbane seed <note>original: "Iusquiame." Henbane is a toxic plant in the nightshade family; in historical cosmetics, its extracts were sometimes used for their supposed sedative or skin-soothing properties despite the risks.</note>, and crush it; place it in a cucurbit <term>Cucurbit: The rounded, gourd-shaped base of a distillation apparatus that holds the liquid to be heated.</term>; pour over it the juice of twelve lemons and one pound of spirit of wine; then distill in a boiling water bath <term>Boiling water bath: Also known as a bain-marie, this technique uses the heat of boiling water to gently and evenly heat a secondary vessel.</term>. One must wash the hands with this water every morning, then put on waxed gloves <note>Waxed or "prepared" gloves were often worn overnight to keep the skin moist and allow cosmetic treatments to penetrate more effectively.</note>.

# CHAPTER XII.

## On Tisanes. <term>Tisane: A medicinal drink or herbal infusion, often made by boiling grains, fruits, or herbs in water to extract their restorative properties.</term>

### Tisane for gaining weight. <note>In the 17th century, "fattening" treatments were sought after because a plump figure was a sign of health and high social status, while thinness was often associated with disease or poverty.</note>

TAKE ivory shavings and hartshorn <note>original: "corne de Cerf." Shavings from deer antlers were a common source of gelatin and were believed to provide strength and nourishment.</note>, one ounce of each; half an ounce of yellow amber; four ounces of Damascus raisins, from which you shall remove the seeds; one handful of wheat grains: boil everything in four pints of water, until reduced by one pint; then filter. One must drink three or four glasses of this tisane every day, and continue for forty days. Before using it, it is necessary to be purged <note>Purging, or the use of laxatives to "clear the humors," was a standard preparatory step in early modern medicine before starting a new treatment.</note>.

### Another tisane which gains weight, and induces sleep.

TAKE oat grits and barley flour, one pound of each; six Pippin apples <note>original: "pommes de Reinettes." A variety of apple prized for its flavor and used in medicine for its supposedly cooling and moistening effects.</note> cut into slices; put everything in a vessel

This tisane, besides making one sleep and gain weight, moistens and refreshes <note>In humoral medicine, "moistening" and "refreshing" (cooling) were used to counteract "dry" or "hot" imbalances in the body, which were thought to cause thinness and insomnia.</note>. It is good for old people and for the young.

### *Another tisane for the same purpose.*

ONE must take wheat, oats, and barley, a handful of each; roots of Water Lily <note>original: "Nenuphar." Water lily roots were historically used in medicine for their calming and cooling properties.</note> and Chicory, well cleaned, two ounces of each; half a pound of Narbonne honey <note>Narbonne honey was a highly prized, light-colored honey from southern France, frequently specified in pharmaceutical recipes for its purity.</note>; boil it all in six pints of water until reduced by half; skim and strain through a cloth, and take it as described above.

### CHAPTER XIII.
Manner of cleaning and dressing
gloves.

TAKE thin and supple <note>The original French "pateux" in this context refers to leather that is soft and malleable, likely a high-quality suede or chamois.</note> leather gloves; wash them in spring water fifteen or twenty times in succession, until they leave the water quite clear: for the final time, you shall wash them in Rosewater, and let them dry slowly in the shade: then take fresh egg yolks, and soak the gloves in them, and rub them in such a way that the egg yolks penetrate the gloves through and through; afterwards, soak them in orange flower oil, or Jasmine oil, or in the absence of those, sweet almond oil, or the oil of the four cold seeds <note>In early modern pharmacy, the "four cold seeds" (quatre semences froides) usually referred to the seeds of cucumber, melon, gourd, and pumpkin, prized for their "cooling" and moisturizing properties.</note>: stretch the gloves on a sheet of paper, and let them dry slowly between two papers, and put them in the air once or twice, for fear that they might spoil, and store them in a dry place.

### Another way for gloves.

**PURIFY** your gloves as was stated above, and rub them in fresh egg yolks: afterwards take two ounces of kidskin pomade <note>Original: "pommade de Chevreau." This likely refers to a specific ointment or rendered fat used to keep delicate kidskin leather supple.</note>, one ounce of yellow or white wax, and one ounce of sweet almond oil: melt everything together, and dip the gloves inside, and rub them, and incorporate the said ingredients <note>Original: "drogues." In the 17th century, this term referred to any raw chemical or natural ingredient used in a preparation, not specifically to medicines or narcotics.</note> so well that they penetrate the gloves: afterwards spread them on white paper, and stretch them into the shape you wish for them to remain: pass a wooden roller over them to smooth <note>The French "unir" implies making the surface uniform and flat.</note> them out, and store them in a dry place. If you wish to scent them, you shall add some fragrant oil to the composition, such as orange blossom or jasmine.

->**Powders for the teeth.**<-

ONE can make powder from all the following things, which whiten and strengthen the teeth; namely red sandalwood, dragon’s blood <note>A red resin used for its astringency.</note>, gall nuts <note>Original: "noix de Galle." Highly astringent growths on oak trees caused by wasps, used to tighten the gums.</note>, white and yellow amber <note>Original: "Carabé." An old term for fossilized resin or amber.</note>, mastic, pearls, barley flour, cinnamon, ivory shavings, and hartshorn, coral, aloeswood, tamarisk leaves, sorrel roots, and white wine tartar <note>Original: "Tartre de vin blanc." The acidic sediment from wine barrels, used here as a mild bleaching agent.</note>: all these things reduced to powder, each individually, whiten the teeth: as also burnt bread crust, pumice stone, and calcined alum, and the powder made from stoneware pots, tiles, and bricks <note>These materials acted as harsh abrasives to mechanically scrub the teeth, though such grit would be considered too damaging for enamel by modern standards.</note>. You must wash the mouth after having used these powders with sage water, or mint water, which is excellent for this purpose.

<meta>This page continues the dental health section of the treatise on "Chemistry," moving from powders described on page 327 to potent liquid essences and the preparation of botanical cleaning tools (chew sticks).</meta>

328        *Chemistry*

*Spirits or Essences suitable for the teeth.*

THE SPIRIT of Sulfur, of Vitriol <note>Original: "Vitriol." Usually refers to sulfuric acid. These highly acidic "spirits" were used to chemically bleach teeth but were dangerously corrosive to enamel and gums.</note>, of Sea Salt, of Saltpeter, and of Alum, whiten the teeth and corrode them, and remove cankers <note>Original: "chancres." This likely refers to stubborn tartar or mouth sores.</note>, and make them clear and white. They must be rubbed lightly with a small stick, or roots, as will be described below, because they would corrode and burn the flesh <note>The author acknowledges the danger of these acids, which would destroy the gums if not applied carefully.</note>: the mouth must be washed afterward with warm wine.

*To prepare the roots and wood for rubbing the teeth.*

TAKE small sticks of Ivy, and roots of Marshmallow <note>Original: "Guimauves." Marshmallow roots are fibrous; when boiled and dried, they fray at the end to create a brush-like tool for cleaning.</note>, and boil them in vinegar with a little Salt and Alum: and when the roots begin to soften, and the wood peels, remove them from the fire, and let them dry gently; then use them to rub the teeth after meals.

<summary>This page details the use of corrosive chemical "spirits" to whiten teeth and remove tartar, as well as a method for creating early toothbrushes from marshmallow roots and ivy sticks boiled in vinegar.</summary>
<keywords>dentistry, chemistry, spirits of sulfur, vitriol, marshmallow root, hygiene, tooth whitening, dental tools</keywords>

## CHAPTER XV.

[ornamental line of dots]

### On hair dyes.

**Dye to make the hair blonde.**

TAKE copper filings, Rock salt <note>Original: "Sel gemme." Naturally occurring mineral salt, also known as halite.</note>, half a pound of each; bryony roots <note>Original: "racines de Coulevrée." This refers to *Bryonia alba*, a climbing plant often used in early medicine and cosmetics.</note> one pound: cut the roots, and crush them, and place them in a retort <note>Original: "Cornuë." A classic alchemical vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation.</note> along with the salt and filings: let them infuse for one night, then distill over a wheel-fire <term>Wheel-fire (feu de rouë): A method of heating where the fire is built in a circle around the vessel to provide even, intense heat.</term> until no more smoke comes out. To use this water, one must dissolve as much gum tragacanth <note>Original: "gomme Adragant." A natural thickening agent derived from the sap of legumes.</note> in rose-water as can be dissolved: take one part of this water, and one part of the distilled water, and heat them slightly; then wet the hair with brushes, or a small paintbrush, and let it dry before combing.

**Another way to dye the hair blonde.**

TAKE bismuth <note>Original: "Estain de glace," literally "tin of ice" or "tin of glass." This was a common early modern term for the metal bismuth.</note>, rock alum, Roman vitriol <note>A sulfate mineral, likely copper or iron sulfate.</note>, yellow sulfur—

[fragment of a horizontal line]

*Dye to make the hair black.*
TAKE one pound of gall nuts <note>original: "noix de Galle." Highly astringent growths from oak trees, rich in tannins and traditionally used to make permanent black inks and dyes.</note>, cut them into pieces, and boil them in olive oil until they are tender: dry them, and crush them very well, and make them into a powder: mix with an equal part of willow charcoal powder <note>original: "charbon de Sault." You must boil the whole mixture in twelve pounds of water, until the ingredients remain in the consistency of an oin— <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; "oin-" is the start of "onguent," or ointment.</note>

<meta>This page concludes the section on hair treatments in Marie Meurdrac’s "Charitable and Easy Chemistry," following the recipes for depilatory waters on the previous page. This page focuses on scalp health and styling.</meta>

334 *Charitable & Easy Chemistry.*

->*Ointment to remove the scurf that*<-
->*occurs at the root of the*<-
->*hair.*<-

TAKE half a pound of hog’s lard, melt it in a small earthenware pot: incorporate flowers of Sulfur <term>Flowers of Sulfur (fleurs de Soufre): A fine, yellow powder produced by the sublimation of sulfur, traditionally used in medicine to treat skin diseases like mange or psoriasis.</term>, and calcined Alum <term>Calcined Alum (Alun calciné): Alum that has been heated to remove its water content, making it a more powerful astringent and drying agent.</term>, one ounce of each: let it come to a boil, then strain and squeeze it out. One must rub the head with this ointment two or three times, leaving two or three days in between. <note>The "flour" (farine) described here refers to dandruff or seborrheic dermatitis, which sulfur and alum would effectively treat due to their antifungal and astringent properties.</note>

*Water to make the hair curl.*

TAKE one ounce of gum Elemi <term>Gum Elemi (gomme Elemy): A fragrant resin derived from various trees in the *Burseraceae* family; it was used in early cosmetics as a fixative and to provide structure.</term>, and let it soak in one pound of Rosewater, which you shall boil for half a quarter of an hour: when it is cold, you must moisten the hair with it, then put it into curling papers <term>Curling papers (papillottes): Small pieces of paper or fabric used to wrap bundles of hair to create curls, often worn overnight.</term>, or under a cap.

->*THE END.*<-
